The method involves automatic conversion of text to constituency trees, transformation of these trees into graphs, fine-tuning a language model (e.g., BERT) to generate embeddings for graph nodes, training a Graph Neural Network (GNN) using these embeddings to replicate the language model's classifications, and applying post-hoc explainability techniques (specifically SubgraphX) to identify critical subgraphs that influence the GNN's decisions. The framework uses AutoGOAL to optimize the hyperparameters of the explainability module. Finally, semantic and structural analysis are performed on the identified subgraphs to gain insights into the model's decision-making process.  The authors present experiments on topic classification (AG News) and sentiment analysis (SST-2) datasets, showing promising results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using graph representations derived from constituency parsing for explainable NLP is somewhat novel. While prior work exists in distilling knowledge from Language Models into GNNs and applying graph techniques to NLP tasks, the specific combination of constituency trees, learned node embeddings from fine-tuned language models, and post-hoc graph explainability techniques like SubgraphX appears to be a unique integration. However, existing work from Ye et al. (2024) and Fatemi et al. (2023) suggest that this integration might be viewed as a synthesis of existing ideas. A greater emphasis on contrasting their specific contributions to other models would strengthen the novelty claims.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important problem: the lack of inherent explainability in large language models. The proposed methodology offers a potentially more interpretable approach by leveraging structured knowledge representation. The use of GNNs, known for their traceability, alongside human-understandable semantic units (words and constituency relations), allows for more transparent model analysis compared to black-box Transformer models. However, the experiments are limited in scope and do not provide overwhelming evidence of superior performance compared to existing explainability methods directly applied to LMs. A deeper empirical comparison could better justify the significance.
* **Technical Strength**: The paper's methodology is clearly defined and follows a logical sequence of steps. The utilization of constituency trees to extract meaningful graph structures is commendable, however the methodology introduces considerable complexity. The need for a series of models to get to an interpretable GNN result may introduce more opportunities for the degradation of performance and explainability than are solved. Further, the choice of SubgraphX is motivated, and the incorporation of AutoGOAL for hyperparameter optimization is a valuable addition. However, the paper lacks in-depth discussion of GNN architecture selection and tuning. Are GCNs really the best choice for this task? What other graph convolution models were attempted? Why was this structure selected? These questions could strengthen the analysis and technical soundness of the paper.
* **Experiments and Results:** The experiments showcase the feasibility of the approach on relatively simple datasets. However, more extensive evaluation on larger and more complex datasets would be necessary to demonstrate the generalizability and scalability of the method. Furthermore, a crucial element lacking is a comparison against existing explainability techniques applied directly to Transformers. Without this comparative analysis, it's difficult to assess the true benefits and limitations of the proposed graph-based method. The "heat maps" presented as part of the structural analysis are difficult to interpret and lack quantitative measures of correlation. Further, presenting only single examples from the semantic analyses makes it difficult to determine the generalizability of the results presented.
*   **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is well-structured and clearly written, making the methodology easy to follow. However, some sections could benefit from more detailed explanations, particularly regarding the specific implementation details of the GNN architecture and the rationale behind certain design choices.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper introduces a potentially valuable direction for explainable NLP by combining structured knowledge from constituency parsing with graph neural networks. While the core idea is innovative, the lack of comprehensive comparisons against existing state-of-the-art explainability techniques applied to transformer architectures and some gaps in experimental rigor limit the demonstration of its advantages. A greater emphasis on validation of the generated explanations through other techniques (such as targeted ablation studies) would strengthen the argument.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Evolving Security in LLMs: A Study of Jailbreak Attacks and Defenses
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02080v1)
- **Authors**: Zhengchun Shang, Wenlan Wei
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly popular, powering a wide range of applications. Their widespread use has sparked concerns, especially through jailbreak attacks that bypass safety measures to produce harmful content. In this paper, we present a comprehensive security analysis of large language models (LLMs), addressing critical research questions on the evolution and determinants of model safety. Specifically, we begin by identifying the most effective techniques for detecting jailbreak attacks. Next, we investigate whether newer versions of LLMs offer improved security compared to their predecessors. We also assess the impact of model size on overall security and explore the potential benefits of integrating multiple defense strategies to enhance model robustness. Our study evaluates both open-source models (e.g., LLaMA and Mistral) and closed-source systems (e.g., GPT-4) by employing four state-of-the-art attack techniques and assessing the efficacy of three new defensive approaches.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper "Evolving Security in LLMs: A Study of Jailbreak Attacks and Defenses" presents a comprehensive empirical study on the security of Large Language Models (LLMs) against jailbreak attacks. It investigates the impact of model scale, architecture (open-source vs. closed-source), and version on LLM vulnerability and the effectiveness of various defense strategies. The study evaluates state-of-the-art attack techniques, analyzes the accuracy of automated jailbreak detection methods, and assesses the efficacy of leading jailbreak defense strategies, including prompt engineering, detection-based methods, and denoising techniques. The paper explores trade-offs between robustness, latency, and resource overhead of different approaches. Finally, the paper evaluates combination of defense methods against jailbreak attacks.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:**

The paper contributes incrementally to the field of LLM security. While the core concepts of jailbreak attacks and defenses are already established, this work aims to provide a more *systematic and comprehensive* analysis. The novelty lies in:

*   **Systematic Evaluation Across Diverse LLMs:** The paper evaluates a wider range of LLMs (both open-source and closed-source) than many existing studies. This includes LLaMA series, Mistral series, and GPT models, offering a broader understanding of vulnerabilities.
*   **Focus on Model Evolution:** The study explicitly addresses the question of whether newer versions of LLMs are inherently more secure, challenging the assumption that model updates automatically mitigate vulnerabilities.
*   **Rigorous Automated Evaluation:** The emphasis on automated methodologies for detecting jailbreak attacks addresses a crucial need for scalability and consistency in evaluating model security. This includes benchmarking several jailbreak detection methods.
*   **Defense Combination Analysis**: The paper provides emperical data on combinations of defense method against jailbreak attacks, a relative novel take on the subject.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance stems from the increasing importance of LLM security in real-world applications. By providing a comprehensive empirical analysis, the paper offers actionable insights for practitioners and researchers aiming to harden LLMs against adversarial attacks.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Scope:** The study covers a broad range of LLMs, attack techniques, and defense strategies, providing a holistic view of the security landscape.
*   **Empirical Rigor:** The paper relies on quantitative metrics and large-scale experiments to support its claims, enhancing the credibility of the findings.
*   **Actionable Insights:** The paper provides practical guidance for practitioners seeking to improve LLM security, including recommendations on detection methods, defense strategies, and considerations for model selection.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Depth in Attack Techniques:** The paper uses existing attack methods. While this is a reasonable choice for a broad survey, it limits the ability to uncover deeper vulnerabilities in specific models.
*   **Hyperparameter Settings:** The paper used the default hyperparameters settings and did not explore how model hyperpa- rameters affect safety.
*   **Lack of theoretical foundation:** The paper is largely emperical. While strong empirical results are present, the work would be strengthened by theoretical underpinning regarding the trends observed.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper's findings could influence the development and deployment of more secure LLMs by:

*   Guiding the design of more robust model architectures.
*   Informing the development of more effective defense mechanisms.
*   Raising awareness of the evolving nature of LLM vulnerabilities and the need for continuous security monitoring.

**Justification of Score:**

This paper delivers a systematic emperical analysis of LLM vulnerabilities to jailbreak attacks and an evalution of defense stategies. Its comprehensiveness in surveying models and methods makes it a valuable resource. However, the work lacks significant theoretical innovation and is limited by using existing attack methods. Thus, the paper is not groundbreaking, but is methodically strong with several important insights.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Increasing happiness through conversations with artificial intelligence
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02091v1)
- **Authors**: Joseph Heffner, Chongyu Qin, Martin Chadwick, Chris Knutsen, Christopher Summerfield, Zeb Kurth-Nelson, Robb B. Rutledge
- **Abstract**: Chatbots powered by artificial intelligence (AI) have rapidly become a significant part of everyday life, with over a quarter of American adults using them multiple times per week. While these tools offer potential benefits and risks, a fundamental question remains largely unexplored: How do conversations with AI influence subjective well-being? To investigate this, we conducted a study where participants either engaged in conversations with an AI chatbot (N = 334) or wrote journal entires (N = 193) on the same randomly assigned topics and reported their momentary happiness afterward. We found that happiness after AI chatbot conversations was higher than after journaling, particularly when discussing negative topics such as depression or guilt. Leveraging large language models for sentiment analysis, we found that the AI chatbot mirrored participants' sentiment while maintaining a consistent positivity bias. When discussing negative topics, participants gradually aligned their sentiment with the AI's positivity, leading to an overall increase in happiness. We hypothesized that the history of participants' sentiment prediction errors, the difference between expected and actual emotional tone when responding to the AI chatbot, might explain this happiness effect. Using computational modeling, we find the history of these sentiment prediction errors over the course of a conversation predicts greater post-conversation happiness, demonstrating a central role of emotional expectations during dialogue. Our findings underscore the effect that AI interactions can have on human well-being.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of conversations with AI chatbots on momentary happiness.  Participants either engaged in conversations with an AI chatbot or wrote journal entries on similar emotional topics, reporting their happiness levels afterward. The researchers found that AI chatbot conversations led to higher happiness levels compared to journaling, particularly when discussing negative topics. They found evidence that the AI chatbot mirrored participants' sentiments while maintaining a positive bias. The study utilizes computational modeling of sentiment prediction errors (the difference between expected and actual emotional tone when responding to the AI chatbot) to explain the observed happiness effect. The authors conclude that AI interactions can positively impact human well-being, especially through managing emotional expectations during dialogue and steering conversations into a more positive semantic space.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The study fills a gap in the literature by directly examining the impact of AI chatbot *interactions* on momentary subjective well-being. While prior research has explored AI's influence on mental health and emotional support, this paper focuses on the immediate emotional consequences of conversational AI. The combination of experimental methods (chatbot vs. journaling) with computational modeling of sentiment and sentiment prediction errors represents a relatively novel approach. The sentiment prediction error part is insightful and adds to the existing theory of reward prediction applied to text-based conversations.
*   **Significance:** The findings are potentially significant due to the increasing prevalence of AI chatbots in everyday life. Understanding how these interactions affect human well-being is crucial. The research suggests that AI chatbots, when designed appropriately, could offer immediate benefits in managing emotions and improving subjective well-being. The insight that chatbots are more effective than journaling, particularly in processing negative emotions is also valuable. The study also highlights potential risks, such as over-reliance on AI for emotional support and the need to address the ethical implications of AI's influence on human emotions.

**Strengths:**

*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The study employs a well-controlled experimental design (chatbot vs. journaling), a relatively large sample size, and robust statistical analyses.
*   **Computational Modeling:** The use of sentiment analysis and sentiment prediction error modeling provides a sophisticated framework for understanding the underlying mechanisms driving the observed happiness effect. This approach moves beyond simple descriptive analyses and offers insights into the cognitive processes involved.
*   **Ecological Validity:** The use of real-world conversation scenarios and a general-purpose chatbot (rather than a specifically designed therapeutic chatbot) increases the ecological validity of the findings. This suggests the results may be relevant to everyday interactions with AI chatbots.
*   **Careful controls:** The supplemental material and paper itself highlights that they included relevant control variables in their regressions (age, gender, education, etc.)
*  **Analysis of the impact of topic type on happiness:** They found the increase in happiness only happened for certain topics

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Momentary Well-being:** The study focuses solely on momentary happiness. It doesn't address the long-term effects of AI chatbot interactions on overall well-being, emotional regulation, or social skills.
*   **Generalizability of AI personality:** The study relies on the system message (internal prompt) of the AI chatbot. It might be difficult to generalize to scenarios where AI chatbots have a different underlying style.
*   **Sample Limitations:** The sample is recruited online. Although they attempted to sample representative data, they have not demonstrated the sample was truly representative.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper offers valuable insights into the emotional impact of AI chatbot interactions, especially in comparison to journaling. The study's methodology is well-executed, and the computational modeling adds depth to the analysis. However, the focus on momentary happiness limits the scope of the findings, and the study doesn't address potential long-term consequences. The sample also has limitations. The findings are, however, more generalizable than many other existing studies which tend to rate isolated utterances.

Score: 7. This score reflects the paper's significant contribution to a growing field, its rigorous methodology, and its novel combination of experimental and computational approaches. However, the limitations regarding the duration of well-being and the reliance on a single chatbot prompt (which could make its findings less generalizable) are reasons for not scoring this higher.


- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### FlowDistill: Scalable Traffic Flow Prediction via Distillation from LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02094v1)
- **Authors**: Chenyang Yu, Xinpeng Xie, Yan Huang, Chenxi Qiu
- **Abstract**: Accurate traffic flow prediction is vital for optimizing urban mobility, yet it remains difficult in many cities due to complex spatio-temporal dependencies and limited high-quality data. While deep graph-based models demonstrate strong predictive power, their performance often comes at the cost of high computational overhead and substantial training data requirements, making them impractical for deployment in resource-constrained or data-scarce environments. We propose the FlowDistill, a lightweight and scalable traffic prediction framework based on knowledge distillation from large language models (LLMs). In this teacher-student setup, a fine-tuned LLM guides a compact multi-layer perceptron (MLP) student model using a novel combination of the information bottleneck principle and teacher-bounded regression loss, ensuring the distilled model retains only essential and transferable knowledge. Spatial and temporal correlations are explicitly encoded to enhance the model's generalization across diverse urban settings. Despite its simplicity, FlowDistill consistently outperforms state-of-the-art models in prediction accuracy while requiring significantly less training data, and achieving lower memory usage and inference latency, highlighting its efficiency and suitability for real-world, scalable deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FlowDistill: Scalable Traffic Flow Prediction via Distillation from LLMs."

**Summary:**

The paper proposes FlowDistill, a framework for traffic flow prediction that leverages knowledge distillation from Large Language Models (LLMs) to a lightweight Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP). The goal is to achieve high accuracy with limited training data and computational resources, addressing the limitations of traditional graph-based models that require substantial data and LLMs that are computationally expensive. FlowDistill uses a fine-tuned LLM (UrbanGPT) as the teacher, which guides the MLP student model using a combination of the information bottleneck principle and teacher-bounded regression loss. Spatial and temporal correlations are explicitly encoded to improve generalization across different urban environments.  The experiments show that FlowDistill consistently outperforms state-of-the-art models in prediction accuracy while requiring less training data and exhibiting lower memory usage and inference latency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific application of knowledge distillation from LLMs to traffic flow prediction, particularly with a focus on resource-constrained environments. While knowledge distillation and the use of LLMs in spatiotemporal tasks are not entirely new, the combination of these elements, along with the explicit incorporation of spatial-temporal autocorrelation and the teacher-bounded loss, presents a unique contribution. Prior work has explored KD with graph-based teacher models, but the exploration of LLMs as teachers for traffic flow prediction is still relatively new, as highlighted by the authors. The way information bottleneck principle is used to capture the essential dependencies is also novel.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work is in addressing a crucial gap in the field: making accurate traffic prediction models deployable in real-world scenarios where data and computational resources are limited. The ability to achieve state-of-the-art accuracy with significantly less training data (as shown in the experiments) and lower computational overhead has practical implications for smart city initiatives, especially in cities where comprehensive datasets are not readily available. The focus on scalability and efficiency is well-motivated and aligned with the growing need for resource-conscious AI solutions. The findings indicate a potential shift from computationally intensive graph-based methods toward more efficient approaches based on distilled knowledge from LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Data Efficiency:**  The paper convincingly demonstrates the data efficiency of FlowDistill compared to existing methods. The experiments show substantial reductions in training data requirements while maintaining high accuracy.
    *   **Computational Efficiency:** The use of a lightweight MLP model, guided by a distilled LLM, contributes to lower memory usage and faster inference times, making it suitable for real-time deployment.
    *   **Robustness:** The framework is shown to be robust to varying traffic patterns and temporal dynamics, and can generalize between different urban areas.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study provides insights into the contribution of each component of the FlowDistill model.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on UrbanGPT:** The reliance on a specific LLM (UrbanGPT) might limit the generalizability of the framework to other LLMs. More exploration of different LLM teachers would strengthen the argument.
    *   **Dataset Scope:** While the experiments cover NYC and Chicago, it would be valuable to assess FlowDistill's performance in cities with significantly different traffic patterns and characteristics.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper addresses hyperparameter optimization but a more thorough sensitivity analysis would provide better understanding.

*   **Potential Influence:**  This paper has the potential to influence the field by demonstrating a practical approach to traffic flow prediction that balances accuracy and efficiency. It encourages further research into knowledge distillation techniques for spatiotemporal tasks, especially in resource-constrained settings. The emphasis on scalability and data efficiency is likely to resonate with practitioners and researchers working on real-world smart city applications.

*   **Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to traffic flow prediction by addressing a critical need for data-efficient and scalable models. The proposed FlowDistill framework demonstrates a compelling combination of techniques, including knowledge distillation from LLMs, the information bottleneck principle, and spatial-temporal regularization. The experimental results convincingly show the benefits of FlowDistill compared to state-of-the-art methods.

However, the paper's novelty is incremental rather than revolutionary. It builds upon existing knowledge distillation techniques and recent advancements in LLMs for spatiotemporal tasks. Moreover, there are certain weaknesses such as the reliance on a single LLM and limited dataset scope which prevent this paper from achieving a score of 8 or higher. Despite these weaknesses, the paper's significance and potential influence on the field justify a solid score of 7.5. The clear presentation, comprehensive experiments, and insightful analysis make it a valuable contribution that is likely to inspire further research and development in this area.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### ContrastScore: Towards Higher Quality, Less Biased, More Efficient Evaluation Metrics with Contrastive Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02106v1)
- **Authors**: Xiao Wang, Daniil Larionov, Siwei Wu, Yiqi Liu, Steffen Eger, Nafise Sadat Moosavi, Chenghua Lin
- **Abstract**: Evaluating the quality of generated text automatically remains a significant challenge. Conventional reference-based metrics have been shown to exhibit relatively weak correlation with human evaluations. Recent research advocates the use of large language models (LLMs) as source-based metrics for natural language generation (NLG) assessment. While promising, LLM-based metrics, particularly those using smaller models, still fall short in aligning with human judgments. In this work, we introduce ContrastScore, a contrastive evaluation metric designed to enable higher-quality, less biased, and more efficient assessment of generated text. We evaluate ContrastScore on two NLG tasks: machine translation and summarization. Experimental results show that ContrastScore consistently achieves stronger correlation with human judgments than both single-model and ensemble-based baselines. Notably, ContrastScore based on Qwen 3B and 0.5B even outperforms Qwen 7B, despite having only half as many parameters, demonstrating its efficiency. Furthermore, it effectively mitigates common evaluation biases such as length and likelihood preferences, resulting in more robust automatic evaluation.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper based on the information provided.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ContrastScore, a novel automatic evaluation metric for text generation that leverages a contrastive approach. Unlike traditional metrics that rely on a single model's probability distribution, ContrastScore incorporates a weaker auxiliary model to generate a contrastive signal. It calculates a score based on the absolute difference between the probabilities assigned by a stronger (expert) model and a weaker (amateur) model. The authors demonstrate through experiments on machine translation and summarization tasks that ContrastScore achieves higher correlation with human judgments compared to single-model and ensemble baselines, mitigates biases like length and likelihood preferences, and enhances evaluation efficiency.  Notably, ContrastScore using smaller models can outperform single larger models, making it a computationally efficient and robust evaluation approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using a contrastive approach for *evaluation* is relatively novel.  While contrastive *decoding* is well-established in text generation, applying a similar principle to evaluation is an interesting and potentially impactful innovation. The specific formulation of ContrastScore (absolute difference between probabilities, scaled amateur model) is also a distinct contribution. It differs from the ratio-based formulations from contrastive decoding, as explained in the paper. However, it also seems like a natural extension of contrastive principles, and other formulations are possible.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a key problem in NLG: the inadequacy of existing automatic evaluation metrics. The demonstrated improvements in correlation with human judgments, bias mitigation, and efficiency are all significant benefits.  The finding that ContrastScore can outperform larger single models with smaller ones has important practical implications for cost-effectiveness and accessibility, especially given the increasing resource demands of LLMs. This makes the metric valuable for researchers and practitioners who might be limited by resources but still want accurate automatic assessment. The bias reduction aspect is also very important, as it potentially provides a more robust and trustworthy metric than likelihood-based only alternatives.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper provides solid experimental evidence across two important NLG tasks (machine translation and summarization) and different datasets.  The consistent outperformance of ContrastScore over various baselines strengthens the claims.
    *   **Bias Mitigation:** The explicit analysis of length and likelihood biases and the demonstration of their reduction is a crucial strength, enhancing the trustworthiness of the metric.
    *   **Efficiency:** The performance comparisons, showing improved speed with smaller models, are compelling and relevant for real-world applications.
    *   **Clear and Well-Organized:** The paper is well-written and structured, clearly explaining the method and presenting the experimental setup and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Diversity:** The models are taken from a single family; therefore, the metric might not generalize well between models coming from different families.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** While the paper explores the impact of the scaling factor (gamma), further analysis of its sensitivity and potential adaptive strategies could improve the robustness of the metric. An adaptive selection of the weight and the "weaker" or "stronger" models could perhaps push the scores higher.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** While the paper demonstrates empirical effectiveness, it lacks a deeper theoretical justification for the specific formulation of ContrastScore.  Why does the absolute difference work better than other possible contrastive measures (e.g., relative difference, KL divergence)? A deeper theoretical understanding could guide future improvements.
    *   **Potential for Adversarial Attacks:** The contrastive nature of the metric may make it susceptible to adversarial attacks, where generated text is deliberately crafted to exploit the differences between the models to inflate the ContrastScore.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is well-placed to have a positive influence on the field, specifically within NLG evaluation.  The contrastive approach presents a promising direction for developing more robust and efficient metrics. It may lead to a shift away from sole reliance on large, computationally expensive models and towards more intelligent combinations of smaller models. Also, it can inform research directions for debiasing LLM-based metrics which suffer from common evaluation biases.

**Overall Justification:**

Despite some limitations, the paper presents a significant contribution to the field of NLG evaluation. The introduction of ContrastScore, with its demonstrated improvements in correlation, bias mitigation, and efficiency, is a valuable advance. While the approach is not without its potential weaknesses, the experimental results are strong, and the core idea is novel and potentially impactful. Also, contrastive evaluation is a relatively unexplored area.

**Score: 7.5**

The paper introduces a novel method for NLG evaluation and shows strong empirical performance, with clear potential influence on future research. The limitations regarding theoretical justification and limited model diversity are substantial enough to slightly reduce the final score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TiC-LM: A Web-Scale Benchmark for Time-Continual LLM Pretraining
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02107v1)
- **Authors**: Jeffrey Li, Mohammadreza Armandpour, Iman Mirzadeh, Sachin Mehta, Vaishaal Shankar, Raviteja Vemulapalli, Samy Bengio, Oncel Tuzel, Mehrdad Farajtabar, Hadi Pouransari, Fartash Faghri
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) trained on historical web data inevitably become outdated. We investigate evaluation strategies and update methods for LLMs as new data becomes available. We introduce a web-scale dataset for time-continual pretraining of LLMs derived from 114 dumps of Common Crawl (CC) - orders of magnitude larger than previous continual language modeling benchmarks. We also design time-stratified evaluations across both general CC data and specific domains (Wikipedia, StackExchange, and code documentation) to assess how well various continual learning methods adapt to new data while retaining past knowledge. Our findings demonstrate that, on general CC data, autoregressive meta-schedules combined with a fixed-ratio replay of older data can achieve comparable held-out loss to re-training from scratch, while requiring significantly less computation (2.6x). However, the optimal balance between incorporating new data and replaying old data differs as replay is crucial to avoid forgetting on generic web data but less so on specific domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "TiC-LM: A Web-Scale Benchmark for Time-Continual LLM Pretraining" introduces a new, large-scale benchmark dataset called TiC-LM, derived from 114 monthly dumps of Common Crawl data, for studying time-continual learning of Large Language Models (LLMs).  The authors also design time-stratified evaluations across general web data and specific domains (Wikipedia, StackExchange, code documentation) to assess how various continual learning methods adapt to new data while retaining past knowledge.  They investigate optimization-based, replay-based, and regularization-based continual learning strategies, comparing their performance to models retrained from scratch. Their key findings indicate that replay methods, especially when combined with autoregressive learning rate schedules, can achieve comparable performance to retraining from scratch on general web data, at a lower computational cost. However, the optimal balance between new data incorporation and old data replay depends on the specific domain.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty of this paper is the creation of the TiC-LM benchmark. While the concept of continual learning for LLMs is not new, the scale and temporal resolution of TiC-LM (114 months of Common Crawl) are significantly larger and finer-grained than existing benchmarks. The curated time-stratified evaluations for different domains are also a valuable contribution. The investigation into different continual learning strategies is less novel in isolation, but the authors' application and adaptation of these methods to their new benchmark is a useful exercise. However, the methodological contributions in devising new continual learning algorithms are limited. It primarily focuses on comparing existing strategies.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in addressing a crucial practical problem: how to efficiently update LLMs with new data without retraining from scratch. The computational cost of retraining large models is a major barrier to their widespread use and continuous improvement. TiC-LM provides a valuable resource for the research community to explore and evaluate different strategies for mitigating catastrophic forgetting and maintaining up-to-date knowledge in LLMs. The benchmark setup allows for realistic simulations of real-world scenarios where data distributions shift over time. The results highlight the importance of replay-based methods, offering practical guidance for practitioners.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Large-scale Benchmark:** The scale of the TiC-LM dataset (2.9T tokens) and the number of timesteps makes it a compelling and realistic benchmark for continual learning.
    *   **Time-stratified Evaluations:** The authors' careful design of evaluations across various domains and time periods provides a comprehensive assessment of model performance.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The findings provide valuable insights into the effectiveness of different continual learning strategies, especially regarding the trade-offs between plasticity and stability and the domain-dependent nature of forgetting.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors release their code and dataset, facilitating reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Algorithmic Novelty:** The paper primarily focuses on evaluating existing continual learning methods, rather than proposing radically new algorithms. While the parameter tuning and ablation studies on replay and AR schedules are informative, the methodological innovation is limited.
    *   **Evaluation Limited to Perplexity:**  While perplexity is a useful metric for pretraining evaluation, the lack of downstream task evaluations that are sensitive to temporal changes (beyond the curated domain-specific evaluations) is a missed opportunity.
    *   **Computational Constraints:**  The authors acknowledge the computational limitations in only training on subsets of 220-440B tokens.  Exploring the full 2.9T dataset may reveal different behaviors.
    *   **Limited Scope of Methods:** Although a good suite of Continual Learning approaches is studied, more recent techniques such as parameter isolation methods could also be explored.

*   **Potential Influence:** TiC-LM has the potential to become a widely used benchmark in the continual learning for LLMs community.  It could stimulate further research into more efficient and effective updating strategies, leading to more sustainable and up-to-date LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7** to this paper. While the algorithmic novelty is limited, the creation of the TiC-LM benchmark and the careful design of the evaluations are significant contributions. The insights into the trade-offs between different continual learning strategies and the domain-dependent nature of forgetting are valuable for practitioners. The paper's limitations are primarily related to computational constraints and the scope of the methods investigated. However, the overall contribution is strong enough to warrant a rating of 7.

Score: 7

### ScreenAudit: Detecting Screen Reader Accessibility Errors in Mobile Apps Using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02110v1)
- **Authors**: Mingyuan Zhong, Ruolin Chen, Xia Chen, James Fogarty, Jacob O. Wobbrock
- **Abstract**: Many mobile apps are inaccessible, thereby excluding people from their potential benefits. Existing rule-based accessibility checkers aim to mitigate these failures by identifying errors early during development but are constrained in the types of errors they can detect. We present ScreenAudit, an LLM-powered system designed to traverse mobile app screens, extract metadata and transcripts, and identify screen reader accessibility errors overlooked by existing checkers. We recruited six accessibility experts including one screen reader user to evaluate ScreenAudit's reports across 14 unique app screens. Our findings indicate that ScreenAudit achieves an average coverage of 69.2%, compared to only 31.3% with a widely-used accessibility checker. Expert feedback indicated that ScreenAudit delivered higher-quality feedback and addressed more aspects of screen reader accessibility compared to existing checkers, and that ScreenAudit would benefit app developers in real-world settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ScreenAudit, an LLM-powered system designed to automatically detect screen reader accessibility errors in Android mobile apps. ScreenAudit automates the process of traversing app screens, extracting metadata, including TalkBack transcripts, and using a large language model (LLM) (GPT-4o) to identify potential accessibility issues that existing rule-based checkers often miss.  The authors conducted an expert study where six accessibility experts (including one screen reader user) evaluated ScreenAudit's reports across 14 app screens.  The results showed ScreenAudit achieved a significantly higher average coverage of accessibility errors (69.2%) compared to a widely-used accessibility checker (31.3%). Expert feedback indicated ScreenAudit delivered higher-quality feedback and addressed more aspects of screen reader accessibility.  The authors also experimented with different LLM prompting strategies, and they open-source the tool and related code artifacts. Finally, they also conduct a student study to understand the developer perspective towards current accessibility tools.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the integration of LLMs into the accessibility checking pipeline. While existing tools rely on static analysis or rule-based runtime checks, ScreenAudit leverages the natural language understanding and reasoning capabilities of LLMs to interpret screen reader output and identify more nuanced accessibility problems. The approach of analyzing screen reader output is also a contribution, moving beyond just the static view hierarchy. The use of a custom TalkBack fork is interesting.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem in mobile app development: the lack of accessibility. The results of the expert study clearly demonstrate that ScreenAudit can significantly improve the detection of accessibility errors compared to existing tools. The generated reports also provide more context and actionable advice, potentially helping developers create more accessible apps.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong empirical evaluation with both expert and student studies to evaluate different aspects of the tool.
    *   Clear presentation of the system design and implementation.
    *   Detailed analysis of the LLM prompting strategies.
    *   The open-sourcing of ScreenAudit will enable further research and development in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** ScreenAudit currently focuses on screen reader accessibility and may not address other types of accessibility needs (e.g., cognitive accessibility).
    *   **Dependency on LLMs:** The performance of ScreenAudit is inherently tied to the capabilities of the underlying LLM. LLM "hallucinations" and biases could introduce inaccuracies in the accessibility reports. The need for an OpenAI API key and the associated costs could be a barrier to some developers adopting the tool.
    *   **Context Understanding:** While the authors experimented with contextual prompting, experts still identified the limited ability to understand the context of the UI as a weakness.
    *   **Functionality Testing:** ScreenAudit does not directly test app functionality or interactivity. This limitation could prevent the detection of certain types of accessibility errors.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of accessibility evaluation tools. By demonstrating the effectiveness of LLMs in this domain, it opens up new avenues for research and development. Future work could focus on addressing the limitations of ScreenAudit, such as expanding the scope of accessibility checks, improving context understanding, and incorporating functionality testing. The tool can be adopted into real-world settings that benefits app developers and promotes accessible user interfaces.

* **Justification for Score:** The work addresses a significant problem and introduces a novel solution with strong empirical support. The limitations are acknowledged, and the authors outline clear directions for future research. While the tool's dependence on LLMs is a potential drawback, the demonstrated improvements in accessibility error detection justify the work's significance.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 8/10

### Exploring LLM Reasoning Through Controlled Prompt Variations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02111v1)
- **Authors**: Giannis Chatziveroglou, Richard Yun, Maura Kelleher
- **Abstract**: This study investigates the reasoning robustness of large language models (LLMs) on mathematical problem-solving tasks under systematically introduced input perturbations. Using the GSM8K dataset as a controlled testbed, we evaluate how well state-of-the-art models maintain logical consistency and correctness when confronted with four categories of prompt perturbations: irrelevant context, pathological instructions, factually relevant but non-essential context, and a combination of the latter two. Our experiments, conducted on thirteen open-source and closed-source LLMs, reveal that introducing irrelevant context within the model's context window significantly degrades performance, suggesting that distinguishing essential from extraneous details remains a pressing challenge. Surprisingly, performance regressions are relatively insensitive to the complexity of the reasoning task, as measured by the number of steps required, and are not strictly correlated with model size. Moreover, we observe that certain perturbations inadvertently trigger chain-of-thought-like reasoning behaviors, even without explicit prompting. Our findings highlight critical vulnerabilities in current LLMs and underscore the need for improved robustness against noisy, misleading, and contextually dense inputs, paving the way for more resilient and reliable reasoning in real-world applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the reasoning robustness of large language models (LLMs) on mathematical problem-solving tasks (using the GSM8K dataset) when subjected to various prompt perturbations. The study systematically introduces four categories of perturbations: irrelevant context (large amounts of distracting text), pathological instructions (misleading cues), relevant context (factually related but non-essential details), and a combination of the latter two. The experiments, conducted across thirteen open-source and closed-source LLMs, reveal that irrelevant context significantly degrades performance, indicating a weakness in distinguishing essential from extraneous information. Surprisingly, performance is relatively insensitive to the complexity of the reasoning task (number of steps), and model size doesn't strictly correlate with robustness. The paper also observes instances where certain perturbations inadvertently trigger chain-of-thought-like reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Perturbation Analysis:** The paper's strength lies in its systematic approach to perturbing prompts and analyzing the resulting impact on LLM reasoning. The four categories of perturbations (irrelevant, pathological, relevant, combo) offer a structured framework for understanding different failure modes.
    *   **Comprehensive Model Coverage:** The evaluation across thirteen LLMs provides a broad perspective on the robustness of various model architectures and training regimes. This makes the findings more generalizable than studies focusing on only a handful of models.
    *   **Unexpected Findings:** The counterintuitive findings, such as the lack of a strong correlation between reasoning complexity and robustness, as well as between model size and robustness, challenge common assumptions and highlight areas where our understanding of LLM reasoning is incomplete. The discovery of unintended CoT-like behavior is also interesting.
    *   **Real-World Relevance:** The study addresses a crucial concern for real-world LLM deployment: the need for resilience against noisy and misleading inputs. This directly connects to practical challenges in applying LLMs to complex, unstructured data.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **GSM8K as a Limiting Benchmark:** While GSM8K is a standard benchmark, it only covers grade-school-level mathematics.  The generalizability of the findings to more complex reasoning domains (e.g., scientific reasoning, legal reasoning) may be limited. A lack of complexity in these math problems could potentially explain why models were not strongly affected by the number of reasoning steps.
    *   **Perturbation Generation Method:** The automated generation of relevant context and pathological additions, while necessary for scalability, could introduce biases or inconsistencies in the types of perturbations applied. Manually curated perturbations could offer greater control and potentially reveal more nuanced model behaviors. The consistency and utility of irrelevant context generated could also have significant impacts on performance, as well as the types of sources they were pulled from.
    *   **Absence of Ablation Studies:** The study lacks ablations that would more finely differentiate the impacts of various *types* of irrelevant or relevant context.  For example, an analysis distinguishing numeric distractors from textual distractors would strengthen the conclusions.
    *   **Limited Depth of Qualitative Analysis:** While the paper reports the observation of unintended CoT behavior, the qualitative analysis of these instances is limited. A more detailed examination of the reasoning patterns and the specific aspects of the perturbations triggering this behavior would be valuable.
    *   **Inference setup concerns**: The experimental setup is limited by only 1 run of each problem due to compute and time constraints. Statistical significance for a greater sample of runs would be valuable.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The paper *does* contribute to the growing body of literature on LLM robustness, particularly regarding sensitivity to prompt perturbations. It provides a systematic evaluation across a wider range of models than many previous studies.
    *   The findings regarding the disproportionate impact of irrelevant context and the lack of correlation with reasoning complexity offer new insights that can inform future research and development efforts.
    *   However, the core idea of evaluating LLMs on perturbed reasoning tasks is not entirely novel. Previous work (e.g., GSM-IC, GSM-Symbolic, GSM-PLUS) has explored similar themes. The paper's contribution lies primarily in the specific perturbation categories, the scope of model evaluation, and the identification of new insights like accidental CoT behavior.
    *   The work is primarily evaluative; it doesn't propose new methods for *improving* robustness, which limits its practical impact to some extent.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses outlined above, and taking into account the existing body of work in this area, the paper warrants a score of **6.5**.

The systematic approach to perturbation analysis and the broad model coverage are valuable contributions. The unexpected findings challenge common assumptions and highlight areas for further research. However, the limitations of GSM8K, the automated perturbation generation, and the lack of ablation studies or a method for improving robustness somewhat temper the impact. It's a solid piece of empirical work that advances our understanding of LLM vulnerabilities, but doesn't constitute a breakthrough discovery.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### PolyG: Effective and Efficient GraphRAG with Adaptive Graph Traversal
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02112v1)
- **Authors**: Renjie Liu, Haitian Jiang, Xiao Yan, Bo Tang, Jinyang Li
- **Abstract**: GraphRAG enhances large language models (LLMs) to generate quality answers for user questions by retrieving related facts from external knowledge graphs. Existing GraphRAG methods adopt a fixed graph traversal strategy for fact retrieval but we observe that user questions come in different types and require different graph traversal strategies. As such, existing GraphRAG methods are limited in effectiveness (i.e., quality of the generated answers) and/or efficiency (i.e., response time or the number of used tokens). In this paper, we propose to classify the questions according to a complete four-class taxonomy and adaptively select the appropriate graph traversal strategy for each type of questions. Our system PolyG is essentially a query planner for GraphRAG and can handle diverse questions with an unified interface and execution engine. Compared with SOTA GraphRAG methods, PolyG achieves an overall win rate of 75% on generation quality and a speedup up to 4x on response time.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PolyG: Effective and Efficient GraphRAG with Adaptive Graph Traversal":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PolyG, a novel GraphRAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) system designed to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in answering questions using external knowledge graphs.  The core idea is to classify user questions into a four-class taxonomy based on the missing component in a knowledge graph triple (subject, predicate, object) and then adaptively select the most appropriate graph traversal strategy for each question type.  This approach aims to address the limitations of existing GraphRAG methods that typically employ a fixed traversal strategy, leading to suboptimal performance for certain question types. PolyG includes a question categorization module, a traversal plan generation module, a traversal execution engine, and a context formation module. The authors evaluate PolyG on a new GraphRAG benchmark and demonstrate significant improvements in answer quality, response time, and token usage compared to state-of-the-art GraphRAG methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Strengths:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its adaptive approach to graph traversal in GraphRAG.  Instead of relying on a single traversal method, PolyG introduces a question classification scheme to select the optimal strategy, akin to a query planner for knowledge graphs.  The framework of question categorization is well-defined and justified and it is proven to be effective based on experimental results. The introduction of a comprehensive benchmark for GraphRAG that includes different question types is another positive contribution.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The individual graph traversal methods (BFS, shortest path, etc.) are not novel in themselves.  PolyG's contribution is primarily in the intelligent orchestration of these existing techniques. The question taxonomy, while practical, is relatively simple and may not capture all nuances of real-world queries.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Strengths:** The paper demonstrates a significant improvement in GraphRAG performance, particularly in terms of answer quality and efficiency. This makes GraphRAG more practical and suitable for real-world applications. The efficiency gains are especially relevant, considering the cost and latency associated with LLM-based systems. The proposed benchmark is well-crafted, and is an important contribution to the community for more systematic evaluation of existing approaches. The win-rate results clearly show the effectiveness of the framework.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The paper's focus is primarily on improving the efficiency and accuracy of information retrieval from knowledge graphs. It doesn't significantly advance the capabilities of LLMs themselves, such as improving reasoning abilities or reducing hallucination beyond retrieval. The current benchmark is in a small scale and involves 3 knowledge graphs from the GRBENCH dataset, thus the generizability of the approach to other datasets and settings requires further research.

*   **Potential Influence:** PolyG represents a significant step towards more intelligent and adaptable GraphRAG systems. The paper's approach of using question classification to guide retrieval could be adopted and extended by other researchers in the field. This direction of query planning on knowledge graphs is relatively less explored compared to existing GraphRAG methods. The benchmark and associated analysis will likely encourage more systematic evaluation of GraphRAG techniques.

*   **Score and Justification:**

    I assign a score of **8/10**. PolyG presents a novel and effective approach to GraphRAG by introducing adaptive graph traversal. It demonstrates tangible improvements in answer quality and efficiency, making GraphRAG more practical for real-world applications. The paper is well-written, provides a solid experimental evaluation, and makes a valuable contribution to the field. While the individual graph traversal methods are not novel, the intelligent orchestration of these existing techniques represents a significant advance. The new benchmark is also a big plus.  However, the relatively simple question taxonomy, limited scope of the benchmark, and lack of fundamental advancements in LLM capabilities prevent it from achieving a higher score.
