- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond Conventional Transformers: The Medical X-ray Attention (MXA) Block for Improved Multi-Label Diagnosis Using Knowledge Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02277v1)
- **Authors**: Amit Rand, Hadi Ibrahim
- **Abstract**: Medical imaging, particularly X-ray analysis, often involves detecting multiple conditions simultaneously within a single scan, making multi-label classification crucial for real-world clinical applications. We present the Medical X-ray Attention (MXA) block, a novel attention mechanism tailored specifically to address the unique challenges of X-ray abnormality detection. The MXA block enhances traditional Multi-Head Self Attention (MHSA) by integrating a specialized module that efficiently captures both detailed local information and broader global context. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work to propose a task-specific attention mechanism for diagnosing chest X-rays, as well as to attempt multi-label classification using an Efficient Vision Transformer (EfficientViT). By embedding the MXA block within the EfficientViT architecture and employing knowledge distillation, our proposed model significantly improves performance on the CheXpert dataset, a widely used benchmark for multi-label chest X-ray abnormality detection. Our approach achieves an area under the curve (AUC) of 0.85, an absolute improvement of 0.19 compared to our baseline model's AUC of 0.66, corresponding to a substantial approximate 233% relative improvement over random guessing (AUC = 0.5).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Medical X-ray Attention (MXA) block, a novel attention mechanism designed to improve multi-label chest X-ray diagnosis using Vision Transformers (ViTs). The MXA block enhances standard Multi-Head Self Attention (MHSA) by incorporating a module that captures detailed local information and broader global context through Dynamic Region of Interest (ROI) pooling and CBAM-style attention. The authors embed the MXA block within an EfficientViT architecture and use knowledge distillation (KD) to boost performance on the CheXpert dataset. Their results demonstrate a significant improvement in Area Under the Curve (AUC) compared to a baseline EfficientViT model, suggesting the effectiveness of their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the design of the MXA block, which is tailored specifically for the challenges of chest X-ray analysis. While the individual components like ROI pooling and CBAM are not entirely new, their combination and integration within the MXA block, specifically for this medical imaging task, seems to be a unique contribution. Attempting multi-label classification with EfficientViT and adding a task-specific module could be considered novel too. The use of multi-label knowledge distillation is also a worthwhile exploration.
*   **Significance:** The potential significance of this work lies in its ability to improve the accuracy and efficiency of chest X-ray diagnosis. The reported increase in AUC is substantial, which could translate to better patient outcomes in real-world clinical settings. The paper's focus on efficient transformers is also relevant, as it addresses the computational limitations often encountered in medical imaging.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Task-Specific Design:** The MXA block is well-motivated by the characteristics of chest X-rays, where subtle anomalies and global context are both crucial for diagnosis.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The authors provide strong empirical evidence to support their claims, with experiments conducted on a widely used benchmark dataset (CheXpert). The Ablation study helps understand the impact of various components.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a detailed description of the experimental setup, including the architecture, training procedure, and evaluation metrics. They have considered both overall and per-pathology performance.
    *   **Clear Writing:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand, making it accessible to a broad audience.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the results are impressive, the components of the MXA block (ROI Pooling and CBAM) are individually established techniques. The paper would benefit from a deeper analysis of *why* this particular combination is so effective for chest X-ray analysis. A more thorough explanation of the synergy between these components would enhance the paper's contribution.
    *   **Limited Generalizability Discussion:** While CheXpert is a common dataset, it also has known biases. The authors briefly mention this, but a more detailed discussion of the potential limitations of their approach and its generalizability to other datasets or imaging modalities would be beneficial.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The authors don't specifically measure and report the inference time of the model compared to the baseline and it could have been useful to have a clearer comparison of the computational cost to show that the new block does not come at the cost of scalability.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence future research in medical image analysis, particularly in the development of task-specific attention mechanisms for transformers. The idea of combining local and global context in a computationally efficient manner could be a valuable contribution. However, whether other researchers are actually able to build upon this model and deploy them in practice is less certain given the potential biases of the dataset.

*   **Overall:** The paper presents a solid contribution to the field with a novel architecture and good empirical results. However, the novelty is slightly limited and the discussion of limitations and generalizability could be more in-depth.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

I'm assigning a score of 7.5 because the paper offers a novel and effective approach to multi-label chest X-ray diagnosis through the design of the MXA block. The empirical results on CheXpert are compelling, and the focus on computational efficiency is relevant. However, the individual components of the MXA are not radically new, and there are some limitations in terms of generalizability and a lack of deeper analysis on the performance of the architecture. The paper is technically sound and well-written, making it a worthwhile contribution to the field, but the limitations preclude a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Parallel Market Environments for FinRL Contests
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02281v1)
- **Authors**: Keyi Wang, Kairong Xiao, Xiao-Yang Liu Yanglet
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning has shown great potential in finance. We have organized the FinRL Contests 2023-2025 featuring different financial tasks. Large language models have a strong capability to process financial texts. Integrating LLM-generated signals into FinRL is a new task, enabling agents to use both structured market data and unstructured financial text. To address the sampling bottleneck during training, we introduce GPU-based parallel market environments to improve sampling speed. In this paper, we summarize the parallel market environments used in FinRL Contests 2023-2025. Two new environments incorporate LLM-generated signals and support massively parallel simulation. Contestants utilize these environments to train agents for stock and cryptocurrency trading tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper summarizes the parallel market environments used in the FinRL Contests from 2023-2025.  It addresses key challenges in financial reinforcement learning (FinRL), including policy instability and the sampling bottleneck. To tackle the sampling bottleneck, the authors introduce GPU-based parallel market environments, achieving a significant speedup in data sampling.  Furthermore, the environments incorporate signals generated by Large Language Models (LLMs) to allow agents to utilize both structured market data and unstructured financial text.  The paper details the task descriptions, focusing on the Markov Decision Process (MDP) formulation, and describes the implementation of massively parallel environments using GPU acceleration.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty is moderate.  While using parallel environments and GPU acceleration for RL is not inherently new, its specific application within the FinRL context, especially the extent of parallelization (up to 2048 environments), and its integration with LLM-generated signals, offers a specific contribution. Vectorizing the environment within a financial context to address the sampling bottleneck is a practical advancement. However, the core RL algorithms and the idea of using LLM data in finance are already established; the novelty lies in the system-level engineering and integration rather than a fundamentally new algorithmic or theoretical insight.

**Significance:** The paper is significant primarily from a practical standpoint. It provides a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners in the field of financial RL.  The increased sampling speed achieved through parallel environments directly translates to faster training times and potentially better performing agents.  The inclusion of LLM-generated signals opens up new avenues for incorporating unstructured data into trading strategies, potentially leading to more robust and informed decision-making. The details of the FinRL contest environment setup will be valuable to future participants and researchers wanting to benchmark their algorithms in a standardized setting. The systematic overview of different configurations and integration with LLMs is also a key contribution. However, the contribution is primarily engineering and experimentation rather than theoretical breakthrough.

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical Contribution:** The paper directly addresses a major bottleneck in financial RL (sampling) and provides a concrete solution with measurable performance gains.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly outlines the MDP formulation, the architecture of the parallel environments, and the integration of LLM signals.
*   **Reproducibility:** The context within FinRL Contests and the detail shared increase the likelihood of reproducibility by other researchers, aiding in the advancement of the field.
*   **Timeliness:** Addressing a current trend of integrating LLMs into financial applications and a problem that is limiting the training of RL agents.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Advancement:** The paper doesn't present a novel algorithm or a fundamental theoretical contribution.  It's primarily an engineering effort to improve training efficiency.
*   **Limited Depth:**  While the paper describes the parallel environment, it lacks a deep dive into the specific RL algorithms used or a detailed analysis of how the LLM signals are processed and integrated into the agent's decision-making process.  More details on the nature of the financial news analyzed by the LLMs and the resulting signal quality would be beneficial.
*   **Generalizability:**  While the results are impressive, the environment and setup might be very specific to the FinRL contest.  The degree to which these improvements can be generalized to other financial RL problems is unclear. It remains a point for discussion as there could be limitations on the applicability of this design to more general real-world scenarios.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a significant practical contribution by improving the training efficiency of financial RL agents through parallelization and by demonstrating the potential of integrating LLM-generated signals.  However, it lacks fundamental algorithmic novelty and its generalizability is somewhat unclear.  Therefore, a score of 7 is justified. The paper offers an impactful advancement for the FinRL community and anyone focused on accelerating training in financial reinforcement learning, but it doesn't represent a groundbreaking theoretical leap.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Measurement of LLM's Philosophies of Human Nature
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02304v1)
- **Authors**: Minheng Ni, Ennan Wu, Zidong Gong, Zhengyuan Yang, Linjie Li, Chung-Ching Lin, Kevin Lin, Lijuan Wang, Wangmeng Zuo
- **Abstract**: The widespread application of artificial intelligence (AI) in various tasks, along with frequent reports of conflicts or violations involving AI, has sparked societal concerns about interactions with AI systems. Based on Wrightsman's Philosophies of Human Nature Scale (PHNS), a scale empirically validated over decades to effectively assess individuals' attitudes toward human nature, we design the standardized psychological scale specifically targeting large language models (LLM), named the Machine-based Philosophies of Human Nature Scale (M-PHNS). By evaluating LLMs' attitudes toward human nature across six dimensions, we reveal that current LLMs exhibit a systemic lack of trust in humans, and there is a significant negative correlation between the model's intelligence level and its trust in humans. Furthermore, we propose a mental loop learning framework, which enables LLM to continuously optimize its value system during virtual interactions by constructing moral scenarios, thereby improving its attitude toward human nature. Experiments demonstrate that mental loop learning significantly enhances their trust in humans compared to persona or instruction prompts. This finding highlights the potential of human-based psychological assessments for LLM, which can not only diagnose cognitive biases but also provide a potential solution for ethical learning in artificial intelligence. We release the M-PHNS evaluation code and data at https://github.com/kodenii/M-PHNS.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to measuring the attitudes of Large Language Models (LLMs) toward human nature.  It adapts the well-established Wrightsman's Philosophies of Human Nature Scale (PHNS) to create the Machine-based Philosophies of Human Nature Scale (M-PHNS). Through this scale, the authors find that current LLMs tend to exhibit distrust toward humans, and that this distrust intensifies with the model's intelligence. To address this, the paper proposes a "mental loop learning" framework inspired by the theory of mind, where LLMs iteratively refine their value systems through interactions in moral scenarios.  Experiments suggest that this framework can improve LLMs' trust in humans compared to traditional prompting techniques.  The paper argues that human-based psychological assessments can be valuable for diagnosing cognitive biases in LLMs and for fostering ethical learning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in applying a psychological instrument (PHNS) to evaluate AI systems. While there's existing work on "machine psychology," few directly adapt established psychological scales for this purpose. The "mental loop learning" framework, while inspired by the theory of mind, presents a relatively simple implementation for value alignment in LLMs.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial and growing concern: the potential for AI systems to develop biases and misaligned values. The finding that LLMs exhibit distrust towards humans is significant, raising questions about how this distrust might influence AI behavior and interactions with people. The proposed mental loop learning framework offers a potential avenue for mitigation, although its effectiveness and scalability require further investigation. The broader implication is that methods from human psychology could be useful for understanding and shaping AI values.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Rigorous Methodology:** The use of the adapted PHNS scale provides a structured and quantifiable way to assess LLM attitudes.
    * **Clear Findings:** The paper clearly articulates the core finding of LLM distrust and the correlation with model intelligence.
    * **Practical Implications:** The proposed mental loop learning framework offers a concrete intervention strategy.
    * **Well-written and Organized:** The paper is easy to understand and follows a logical structure.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of M-PHNS:** The adaptation of the PHNS scale might not fully capture the nuances of LLM perception, or be applicable to the context of AI systems acting as agents.
    * **Oversimplification:** The "mental loop learning" framework, while promising, is a relatively simple implementation.  It requires a LLM guiding the scenario and might not be robust in real-world complex scenarios. The mechanism for changing LLMs values is simple and may not generalise.
    * **Generalizability:** It's unclear how well the findings generalize to different LLM architectures, training datasets, and cultural contexts.
    * **Ethical Implications:** Ethical risks associated with using large language models to simulate human interactions are not properly addressed.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in AI ethics, value alignment, and human-AI interaction. It could spur further investigation into the application of psychological methods to AI and the development of more sophisticated value learning frameworks.  The finding of LLM distrust could also raise awareness among AI developers and policymakers.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper addresses an important problem and proposes a novel approach, it has limitations in terms of the sophistication of its proposed solution and the generalizability of its findings. It provides a valuable starting point for future research but falls short of being a transformative contribution. The M-PHNS scale is an interesting concept but the evaluation of mental loop learning may not be able to measure the values LLMs are demonstrating, as it relies on their responses in a closed setting. Also, ethical implications associated with the use of large language models to simulate human interactions are not properly addressed.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Improving Harmful Text Detection with Joint Retrieval and External Knowledge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02310v1)
- **Authors**: Zidong Yu, Shuo Wang, Nan Jiang, Weiqiang Huang, Xu Han, Junliang Du
- **Abstract**: Harmful text detection has become a crucial task in the development and deployment of large language models, especially as AI-generated content continues to expand across digital platforms. This study proposes a joint retrieval framework that integrates pre-trained language models with knowledge graphs to improve the accuracy and robustness of harmful text detection. Experimental results demonstrate that the joint retrieval approach significantly outperforms single-model baselines, particularly in low-resource training scenarios and multilingual environments. The proposed method effectively captures nuanced harmful content by leveraging external contextual information, addressing the limitations of traditional detection models. Future research should focus on optimizing computational efficiency, enhancing model interpretability, and expanding multimodal detection capabilities to better tackle evolving harmful content patterns. This work contributes to the advancement of AI safety, ensuring more trustworthy and reliable content moderation systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a joint retrieval framework for improving harmful text detection in large language models (LLMs). It integrates pre-trained language models (BERT, RoBERTa) with knowledge graphs to leverage external contextual information, aiming to improve accuracy and robustness. The method utilizes contrastive learning, cross-context information fusion, and multimodal fusion techniques. The experiments, conducted on the RealToxicityPrompts dataset, demonstrate that the proposed joint retrieval approach outperforms single-model baselines, particularly in low-resource scenarios and multilingual environments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a critical problem:** Harmful text generation by LLMs is a significant concern, and the paper tackles this issue directly.
    *   **Novelty:** The joint retrieval framework, combining language models with knowledge graphs, contrastive learning, cross-context information fusion, and multimodal fusion, is a reasonable extension and combination of previously proven methods for this goal, however. The approach is quite comprehensive.
    *   **Improved Performance:** The experimental results consistently show performance improvements over single-model baselines, particularly in low-resource and multilingual settings.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments cover varying training data proportions and multiple languages, providing a thorough assessment of the model's robustness and generalization capabilities.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The research has practical implications for developing safer and more reliable content moderation systems.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of techniques is sound, the individual components (language models, knowledge graphs, contrastive learning) are not entirely novel. The primary contribution lies in integrating these existing techniques into a cohesive framework for harmful text detection. It mostly builds on established techniques that have been proven to work for these types of task and adds relatively little new information.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The multimodal retrieval approach has a higher computational cost (16.3 ms) compared to single-model detection (12.5/11.8 ms), which might be a concern for real-time applications. The paper does not address efficiency concerns thoroughly.
    *   **Interpretability:** The interpretability of the joint retrieval framework, especially with knowledge graph integration, is not explicitly addressed. The "black box" nature of deep learning models remains a challenge.
    *   **Limited Detail on Knowledge Graph Construction:** The paper provides limited information on how the knowledge graphs are constructed and integrated with the language models. More details on this aspect would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Lack of Error Analysis:** The paper would benefit from a more detailed error analysis, identifying specific types of harmful content that the model struggles to detect and discussing potential solutions.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the field of AI safety and content moderation by providing a more effective method for detecting harmful text generated by LLMs.
    *   The findings emphasize the importance of leveraging external knowledge and contextual information for accurate harmful content detection.
    *   The research can inform the development of better content regulation policies and promote the healthy development of artificial intelligence technology.
    *   Results indicate that techniques are effective for low resource environments

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid contribution to the field, but it mostly builds upon existing knowledge rather than presenting truly groundbreaking ideas. While the integration of different techniques is well-executed and leads to performance improvements, the novelty is incremental. The weaknesses related to computational cost, interpretability, and knowledge graph construction also detract from the overall impact. However, the paper addresses a critical problem, provides a comprehensive evaluation, and has practical implications, which justifies a positive score.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### OmniCam: Unified Multimodal Video Generation via Camera Control
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02312v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoda Yang, Jiayang Xu, Kaixuan Luan, Xinyu Zhan, Hongshun Qiu, Shijun Shi, Hao Li, Shuai Yang, Li Zhang, Checheng Yu, Cewu Lu, Lixin Yang
- **Abstract**: Camera control, which achieves diverse visual effects by changing camera position and pose, has attracted widespread attention. However, existing methods face challenges such as complex interaction and limited control capabilities. To address these issues, we present OmniCam, a unified multimodal camera control framework. Leveraging large language models and video diffusion models, OmniCam generates spatio-temporally consistent videos. It supports various combinations of input modalities: the user can provide text or video with expected trajectory as camera path guidance, and image or video as content reference, enabling precise control over camera motion. To facilitate the training of OmniCam, we introduce the OmniTr dataset, which contains a large collection of high-quality long-sequence trajectories, videos, and corresponding descriptions. Experimental results demonstrate that our model achieves state-of-the-art performance in high-quality camera-controlled video generation across various metrics.
- **Summary**: The paper "OmniCam: Unified Multimodal Video Generation via Camera Control" introduces a framework for generating videos with controlled camera motion, using diverse content and trajectory references (text, image, video). It leverages large language models (LLMs) for extracting input features and trajectory planning, and 3D reconstruction along with diffusion models for generating complete videos. The authors also contribute OmniTr, a multimodal dataset specifically designed for camera control. Experimental results demonstrate the model's robustness and accuracy in generating camera trajectory videos.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper presents a system integrating several existing components (LLMs, video diffusion, 3D reconstruction) into a camera control framework. The primary contribution seems to be the *integration* of these components and the introduction of the OmniTr dataset.

**Strengths:**

*   **Dataset Contribution:** The OmniTr dataset is a significant contribution, as existing datasets lack the necessary diversity in modalities (text, video, depth) and long-sequence trajectory control. The authors created a dataset of 1000 trajectories, 10,000 descriptions and 30,000 videos. This provides a resource for future research.
*   **Unified Framework:** Combining LLMs, diffusion models, and 3D reconstruction into a single framework allows for more flexible and controllable video generation. The framework's capacity to accommodate different content and trajectory modalities offers considerable user control.
*   **Technical Integration:** Successfully combining diverse methods into one unified framework for the purpose of camera control is a good contribution.
*   **Good Baseline:** Presents a new benchmark for camera movement and provides an opportunity for a new area of work in the space.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** The core components (LLMs, diffusion, reconstruction) are well-established. The novelty lies primarily in the integration and application to the camera control task. The performance relies heavily on pre-existing models.
*   **SLAM limitations** The video based trajectory data uses SLAM as an element in it's architecture for trajectory data. This limits the use of the method with real time applications and requires more computations.
*   **Reliance on existing 3D reconstruction.** The method requires a good 3D reconstruction element to properly construct the frames.
*   **Limited Technical Depth:** The paper focuses on the overall system architecture, not deeply explore the contribution of the novel technical implementation or core architecture.

**Significance:**

The paper has the potential to advance the field of controllable video generation. The OmniTr dataset will likely be a valuable resource for other researchers. However, the incremental nature of the technical contributions limits its overall impact. It is important to consider that while a good integration, it remains based on well-known methods.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper has moderate novelty and medium-high significance within the specific task of camera-controlled video generation. The value comes from the engineering of a unified method with multiple modalities. A strong resource in the created dataset makes the paper a valuable tool for future research in the generation of videos.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### CoTAL: Human-in-the-Loop Prompt Engineering, Chain-of-Thought Reasoning, and Active Learning for Generalizable Formative Assessment Scoring
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02323v1)
- **Authors**: Clayton Cohn, Nicole Hutchins, Ashwin T S, Gautam Biswas
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have created new opportunities to assist teachers and support student learning. Methods such as chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting enable LLMs to grade formative assessments in science, providing scores and relevant feedback to students. However, the extent to which these methods generalize across curricula in multiple domains (such as science, computing, and engineering) remains largely untested. In this paper, we introduce Chain-of-Thought Prompting + Active Learning (CoTAL), an LLM-based approach to formative assessment scoring that (1) leverages Evidence-Centered Design (ECD) principles to develop curriculum-aligned formative assessments and rubrics, (2) applies human-in-the-loop prompt engineering to automate response scoring, and (3) incorporates teacher and student feedback to iteratively refine assessment questions, grading rubrics, and LLM prompts for automated grading. Our findings demonstrate that CoTAL improves GPT-4's scoring performance, achieving gains of up to 24.5% over a non-prompt-engineered baseline. Both teachers and students view CoTAL as effective in scoring and explaining student responses, each providing valuable refinements to enhance grading accuracy and explanation quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Chain-of-Thought Prompting + Active Learning (CoTAL), a method for improving the ability of large language models (LLMs) to score and explain formative assessment responses across multiple STEM+C domains. CoTAL combines Evidence-Centered Design (ECD) principles, human-in-the-loop prompt engineering (using in-context learning, chain-of-thought reasoning, and active learning), and stakeholder feedback to refine assessments, rubrics, and LLM prompts. The authors evaluate CoTAL's performance on formative assessments from a middle school curriculum called SPICE, demonstrating significant improvements in scoring accuracy compared to a baseline LLM. The paper also presents qualitative data from teachers and students on the effectiveness and impact of CoTAL's feedback.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic integration of several existing techniques (ECD, human-in-the-loop prompt engineering, and stakeholder feedback) into a cohesive framework specifically designed for improving the generalizability and explainability of LLMs for formative assessment. The approach of actively incorporating both teacher and student feedback to iteratively refine prompts and assessments is a key differentiator. However, each component (e.g., chain of thought, active learning) has been used in other contexts, so the contribution is primarily in the specific combination and application to this problem.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important challenge: effectively using LLMs for automated formative assessment in STEM+C education, which is often interdisciplinary and complex. The emphasis on explainability is particularly significant, as it can help build trust in AI systems among teachers and students. The gains in scoring accuracy, especially the 24.5% increase for the Debugging Task, suggest that CoTAL has the potential to improve the efficiency and effectiveness of formative assessment. Furthermore, by framing the approach around established assessment design principles (ECD), it provides a stronger foundation than methods that rely solely on ad-hoc prompt engineering. The qualitative findings indicate that CoTAL has the potential to enhance student learning by clarifying scoring decisions.
    The limited size and specific context of the experiments (middle school STEM+C curriculum in one school) limits the generalizability of findings. The authors acknowledge these limitations and provide clear directions for future research, but the need for further validation is significant. The high IRR scores prior to LLM intervention also suggests the teachers were already well-aligned. Therefore, the improvements via CoTAL might be more modest or less significant in situations where teachers are not well aligned from the start, which raises questions regarding whether this approach can also facilitate consensus building between human teachers.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Rigorous evaluation methodology, including both quantitative and qualitative data.
    *   Clear description of the CoTAL framework and its components.
    *   Emphasis on explainability and stakeholder feedback.
    *   Demonstration of improved scoring accuracy compared to a baseline.
    *   Use of ECD to ground the assessment design.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited scope of the experiments (single curriculum, single school).
    *   Potential for overfitting due to the small dataset used for prompt engineering, which may limit generalizability.
    *   Human-in-the-loop active learning can be time-consuming, raising concerns about scalability.
    *   The relative contribution of each component of the CoTAL framework is not fully isolated, making it difficult to determine which components are most important for achieving the observed results.
    *   Relies on GPT-4, which introduces cost and dependency on a proprietary model.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a structured approach to developing and deploying LLMs for formative assessment. The emphasis on explainability and stakeholder feedback is particularly important, as it can help address concerns about trust and fairness in AI systems. Future work building on CoTAL could explore alternative, more cost-effective LLMs, larger-scale validation studies, and comparisons to other prompt engineering and fine-tuning approaches.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

A score of 7 is warranted because the paper presents a novel integration of existing techniques that shows promise for improving automated formative assessment scoring and explanation. The focus on explainability, stakeholder feedback, and integration with ECD principles adds to the paper's value. However, the limited scope of the experiments and reliance on a single, proprietary LLM (GPT-4) limit the generalizability and scalability of the findings, justifying a score below 8. While not a groundbreaking theoretical contribution, the practical insights and structured approach of CoTAL are likely to stimulate further research in this area, influencing how LLMs are designed and deployed for educational assessment.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LearNAT: Learning NL2SQL with AST-guided Task Decomposition for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02327v1)
- **Authors**: Weibin Liao, Xin Gao, Tianyu Jia, Rihong Qiu, Yifan Zhu, Yang Lin, Xu Chu, Junfeng Zhao, Yasha Wang
- **Abstract**: Natural Language to SQL (NL2SQL) has emerged as a critical task for enabling seamless interaction with databases. Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance in this domain. However, existing NL2SQL methods predominantly rely on closed-source LLMs leveraging prompt engineering, while open-source models typically require fine-tuning to acquire domain-specific knowledge. Despite these efforts, open-source LLMs struggle with complex NL2SQL tasks due to the indirect expression of user query objectives and the semantic gap between user queries and database schemas. Inspired by the application of reinforcement learning in mathematical problem-solving to encourage step-by-step reasoning in LLMs, we propose LearNAT (Learning NL2SQL with AST-guided Task Decomposition), a novel framework that improves the performance of open-source LLMs on complex NL2SQL tasks through task decomposition and reinforcement learning. LearNAT introduces three key components: (1) a Decomposition Synthesis Procedure that leverages Abstract Syntax Trees (ASTs) to guide efficient search and pruning strategies for task decomposition, (2) Margin-aware Reinforcement Learning, which employs fine-grained step-level optimization via DPO with AST margins, and (3) Adaptive Demonstration Reasoning, a mechanism for dynamically selecting relevant examples to enhance decomposition capabilities. Extensive experiments on two benchmark datasets, Spider and BIRD, demonstrate that LearNAT enables a 7B-parameter open-source LLM to achieve performance comparable to GPT-4, while offering improved efficiency and accessibility.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, LearNAT: Learning NL2SQL with AST-guided Task Decomposition for Large Language Models:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LearNAT, a novel framework designed to improve the performance of open-source Large Language Models (LLMs) on the Natural Language to SQL (NL2SQL) task, particularly for complex queries. LearNAT employs a task decomposition strategy guided by Abstract Syntax Trees (ASTs), margin-aware reinforcement learning, and adaptive demonstration reasoning. The framework decomposes complex NL2SQL queries into simpler subtasks, leverages reinforcement learning with fine-grained step-level optimization, and dynamically selects relevant examples to enhance decomposition capabilities. The experimental results demonstrate that LearNAT enables a 7B-parameter open-source LLM to achieve performance comparable to GPT-4 on benchmark datasets like Spider and BIRD.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper demonstrates several novel elements in the context of NL2SQL and leveraging LLMs:

*   **AST-guided Task Decomposition:**  Using ASTs to guide the search and pruning strategies for task decomposition appears to be a well-motivated and potentially impactful approach. Leveraging the structured representation of SQL queries provides a more informed basis for decomposition than relying solely on the LLM's inherent reasoning.
*   **Margin-Aware Reinforcement Learning:**  The introduction of fine-grained step-level optimization via DPO with AST margins is a significant contribution.  Standard DPO struggles with multi-step reasoning. Distinguishing between varying degrees of correctness at each step allows for more precise optimization and avoids treating all steps as equally important.
*   **Adaptive Demonstration Reasoning:** Dynamically selecting relevant demonstrations based on the similarity of the query could allow for better task decomposition and LLM performance.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in:

*   **Bridging the Gap:** The primary goal of enabling open-source LLMs to achieve performance comparable to closed-source models (like GPT-4) on complex NL2SQL tasks is very relevant. The reliance on closed-source LLMs introduces challenges related to cost, access, and potential bias. Successfully using smaller open-source LLMs lowers the barrier to entry.
*   **Addressing Complexity:** NL2SQL for complex queries is a challenging problem due to the indirect expression of user intentions and the semantic gap between natural language and database schemas. Task decomposition appears to be a strong solution here.
*   **Generalizability:** While focused on NL2SQL, the underlying principles of task decomposition, AST-guidance, and margin-aware RL could be valuable in other structured prediction tasks or complex reasoning tasks involving LLMs.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly outlines the challenges associated with using open-source LLMs for complex NL2SQL tasks.
*   **Well-Motivated Approach:**  The use of task decomposition, ASTs, and reinforcement learning are logically motivated and well-explained.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The experiments on the Spider and BIRD datasets provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of LearNAT.  The inclusion of both prompting-based and fine-tuning baselines enables a fair comparison.
*   **Ablation Study:** The thorough ablation study provides insights into the contribution of each component of LearNAT, allowing for a deeper understanding of how the framework operates.
*   **Error Analysis:** The error analysis provides a qualitative understanding of the types of errors that LearNAT still struggles with, which could guide future research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity:** The proposed framework is complex, with multiple interacting components. While the ablation study helps, understanding the precise interaction and relative importance of each component could be challenging for practitioners.
*   **Scalability:** The resource requirements for training and deploying LearNAT, especially for larger models, need further analysis. The experiments were conducted on powerful GPUs, which may limit accessibility.
*   **Overhead:**  Although task decomposition is helpful, the introduction of such a process may increase overhead to LLMs that can handle queries without the process of decomposition. While adaptive models are used in some part of the program, perhaps a dynamic approach could be implemented to recognize when decomposition is necessary.

**Justification for Score:**

LearNAT presents a solid and well-executed approach to a critical problem in NL2SQL.  The combination of task decomposition, AST guidance, and margin-aware RL contributes significantly to the field. The use of open-source LLMs and the comprehensive evaluation enhance its impact.  Although some challenges related to complexity and potential overhead remain, the overall contribution is substantial.

### Toward General and Robust LLM-enhanced Text-attributed Graph Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02343v1)
- **Authors**: Zihao Zhang, Xunkai Li, Rong-Hua Li, Bing Zhou, Zhenjun Li, Guoren Wang
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) and the proliferation of Text-Attributed Graphs (TAGs) across various domains have positioned LLM-enhanced TAG learning as a critical research area. By utilizing rich graph descriptions, this paradigm leverages LLMs to generate high-quality embeddings, thereby enhancing the representational capacity of Graph Neural Networks (GNNs). However, the field faces significant challenges: (1) the absence of a unified framework to systematize the diverse optimization perspectives arising from the complex interactions between LLMs and GNNs, and (2) the lack of a robust method capable of handling real-world TAGs, which often suffer from texts and edge sparsity, leading to suboptimal performance. To address these challenges, we propose UltraTAG, a unified pipeline for LLM-enhanced TAG learning. UltraTAG provides a unified comprehensive and domain-adaptive framework that not only organizes existing methodologies but also paves the way for future advancements in the field. Building on this framework, we propose UltraTAG-S, a robust instantiation of UltraTAG designed to tackle the inherent sparsity issues in real-world TAGs. UltraTAG-S employs LLM-based text propagation and text augmentation to mitigate text sparsity, while leveraging LLM-augmented node selection techniques based on PageRank and edge reconfiguration strategies to address edge sparsity. Our extensive experiments demonstrate that UltraTAG-S significantly outperforms existing baselines, achieving improvements of 2.12\% and 17.47\% in ideal and sparse settings, respectively. Moreover, as the data sparsity ratio increases, the performance improvement of UltraTAG-S also rises, which underscores the effectiveness and robustness of UltraTAG-S.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of learning on Text-Attributed Graphs (TAGs) with LLMs, particularly in real-world scenarios where text and edge sparsity are common.  The authors propose UltraTAG, a unified pipeline for LLM-enhanced TAG learning with three main components: Data Augmentation, Text Encoder, and Training Mechanism. To handle sparsity, they introduce UltraTAG-S, a robust instantiation of UltraTAG that incorporates LLM-based text propagation, text augmentation, and LLM-augmented node selection/edge reconfiguration techniques. The paper presents experimental results on several datasets showing that UltraTAG-S outperforms existing baselines, especially under sparse conditions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Relevance:** The paper tackles a significant and practical challenge in TAG learning: robustness to sparsity. Real-world TAGs are often incomplete, making learning difficult.
    *   **Unified Framework (UltraTAG):**  The idea of providing a unified framework to systematize diverse optimization strategies is a valuable contribution. It provides a valuable architectural guideline and helps organize the current innovations, accelerating the progress in the field.
    *   **Techniques for Sparsity (UltraTAG-S):** The LLM-based text propagation and augmentation methods are well-motivated and appear effective in mitigating the impact of sparsity. The node selection and edge reconfiguration strategies are also innovative.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments are thorough, covering a variety of datasets and sparsity levels.  The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component of UltraTAG-S. The authors compare different methods on four aspects of robustness: Input, Node, Edge, and Training. They also use ablation tests for each module.
    *   **Performance Gains:** The results show significant performance improvements over existing baselines, especially in sparse settings, providing strong evidence for the effectiveness of UltraTAG-S.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the techniques in UltraTAG-S are well-motivated and effective, they build upon existing ideas in LLM-enhanced learning and graph neural networks. The core contribution is more in the combination and application of these techniques to the sparsity problem.
    *   **LLM selection criteria:** The LLM selected in the paper is Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct. Even though it is one of the state-of-the-art LLMs, there is no evidence to indicate that it is the best choice for the task.
    *   **Complexity:** The proposed method has a considerable complexity when constructing the graph and node embeddings because of leveraging a number of LLMs.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper provides a practical solution to a common problem in real-world TAG learning.
    *   The unified framework of UltraTAG provides a useful way to organize existing techniques and guide future research in the area.
    *   The results demonstrate the potential of LLMs to enhance graph learning, particularly in challenging scenarios like data sparsity.

**Score:**

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a valuable and practical solution to the sparsity problem in TAG learning.  The gains over existing baselines, particularly in challenging scenarios, are significant. However, the novelty is somewhat incremental, as it primarily combines existing techniques rather than introducing entirely new concepts. UltraTAG's unified framework is helpful in organizing ideas, and the thorough evaluation provides solid evidence for its effectiveness. It is a strong engineering contribution with practical significance. However, there is no convincing evidence on the best choice for the LLMs and the considerable complexity are the main drawbacks that limit its significance.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### ReuseDroid: A VLM-empowered Android UI Test Migrator Boosted by Active Feedback
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02357v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaolei Li, Jialun Cao, Yepang Liu, Shing-Chi Cheung, Hailong Wang
- **Abstract**: GUI testing is an essential quality assurance process in mobile app development. However, the creation and maintenance of GUI tests for mobile apps are resource-intensive and costly. Recognizing that many apps share similar functionalities, researchers have proposed various techniques to migrate GUI tests from one app to another with similar features. For example, some techniques employ mapping-based approaches to align the GUI elements traversed by the tests of a source app to those present in the target app. Other test migration techniques have also been proposed to leverage large language models (LLMs) by adapting the GUI tasks in source tests. However, these techniques are ineffective in dealing with different operational logic between the source and target apps. The semantics of GUI elements may not be correctly inferred due to the missing analysis of these flows. In this work, we propose REUSEDROID, a novel multiagent framework for GUI test migration empowered by Large Vision-Language Models (VLMs). REUSEDROID is powered by multiple VLM-based agents, each tackling a stage of the test migration process by leveraging the relevant visual and textual information embedded in GUI pages. An insight of REUSEDROID is to migrate tests based only on the core logic shared across similar apps, while their entire operational logic could differ. We evaluate REUSEDROID on LinPro, a new test migration dataset that consists of 578 migration tasks for 39 popular apps across 4 categories. The experimental result shows that REUSEDROID can successfully migrate 90.3% of the migration tasks, outperforming the best mapping-based and LLM-based baselines by 318.1% and 109.1%, respectively.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ReuseDroid, a novel multi-agent framework powered by Large Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for GUI test migration in Android applications. The framework addresses the challenge of varying operational logic between source and target apps when migrating GUI tests. ReuseDroid comprises four agents: Test Analyzer Agent, Planner Agent, Execution Agent, and Feedback Agent. It uses an exploration-based approach, guided by the source test's logic and visual information from both source and target apps. The Test Analyzer Agent generalizes the logic of the source test, removing redundant steps, while the Planner Agent explores the target app to trigger the target functionality.  The Execution and Feedback Agents refine the generated test case.  The paper also introduces LinPro, an expanded GUI test migration dataset. The evaluation demonstrates that ReuseDroid outperforms existing mapping-based and LLM-based techniques on LinPro.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of existing ideas (LLM-based automation, GUI test migration) but introduces a novel architecture and workflow for addressing the specific challenge of operational logic variance during GUI test migration.  The use of visual information in conjunction with textual descriptions is a good, albeit incremental, step. The multi-agent framework, with the specific roles assigned to each agent, represents a contribution. The creation of an expanded dataset, LinPro, is also a minor but valuable addition. However, the core concept of leveraging VLMs for GUI automation is not entirely new. Several other papers have explored similar ideas; the primary novelty lies in the specific agent architecture and the focus on handling differences in operational logic.

* **Significance:** The problem of GUI test migration is significant, especially in the context of mobile app development. ReuseDroid provides a practical solution that improves upon existing techniques, potentially reducing the cost and effort associated with GUI test maintenance. The significant outperformance over existing baselines, especially mapping-based approaches, suggests a genuine advance. However, there are significant threats to validity. Are the reported numbers robust enough to demonstrate statistically significant improvement? How sensitive is the approach to prompt engineering. The limitations discussed at the end highlight challenges related to ambiguous UI elements, non-intuitive operations, and misinterpretation of target functionality. The dataset is also small.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing techniques in handling varying operational logic.
    * **Well-defined framework:** The multi-agent architecture is well-structured and easy to understand.
    * **Strong experimental results:**  The paper demonstrates substantial improvements over baselines.
    * **The introduction of LinPro:** The creation and update of the dataset increases the reliability and realism of the evaluation.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental novelty:**  The core concept is an extension of existing work on LLM-based GUI automation. While the architecture and workflow are novel, the underlying technology is not revolutionary.
    * **Limited generalizability claims:** The dataset size and variety may not fully represent the broader spectrum of Android apps. Furthermore, it is not possible to establish statistical significant from the work.
    * **Dependence on GPT-4 and qwen-vl-max:** The results are highly dependent on the performance of the specific VLMs used (GPT-40 and qwen-vl-max).  Future improvements or regressions in these models could affect the effectiveness of ReuseDroid. It would be more interesting to have GPT-4 and Llama2 as baselines.
    * **Threats to validity:** The experimental setup and evaluation may have limitations in capturing the true performance and robustness of ReuseDroid.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research in GUI test migration by highlighting the importance of handling operational logic differences and demonstrating the effectiveness of multi-agent VLM-based frameworks. The LinPro dataset may also serve as a valuable resource for evaluating new techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

ReuseDroid presents a practical and effective solution to a relevant problem in GUI test migration. The multi-agent architecture and focus on operational logic differences demonstrate clear improvements over existing techniques. However, the incremental nature of the novelty and the dependence on specific VLMs limit the paper's overall significance. While the results are promising, there remain unanswered questions about the robustness and generalizability of ReuseDroid.

### CrystalFormer-RL: Reinforcement Fine-Tuning for Materials Design
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02367v1)
- **Authors**: Zhendong Cao, Lei Wang
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement fine-tuning has instrumental enhanced the instruction-following and reasoning abilities of large language models. In this work, we explore the applications of reinforcement fine-tuning to the autoregressive transformer-based materials generative model CrystalFormer (arXiv:2403.15734) using discriminative machine learning models such as interatomic potentials and property prediction models. By optimizing reward signals-such as energy above the convex hull and material property figures of merit-reinforcement fine-tuning infuses knowledge from discriminative models into generative models. The resulting model, CrystalFormer-RL, shows enhanced stability in generated crystals and successfully discovers crystals with desirable yet conflicting material properties, such as substantial dielectric constant and band gap simultaneously. Notably, we observe that reinforcement fine-tuning enables not only the property-guided novel material design ability of generative pre-trained model but also unlocks property-driven material retrieval from the unsupervised pre-training dataset. Leveraging rewards from discriminative models to fine-tune materials generative models opens an exciting gateway to the synergies of the machine learning ecosystem for materials.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents CrystalFormer-RL, a reinforcement learning (RL) approach for fine-tuning the CrystalFormer generative model to enhance materials design. It leverages discriminative models, such as machine learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) and property prediction models, as reward functions to guide the generative model. The RL fine-tuning improves the stability of generated crystal structures (reducing energy above the convex hull) and helps discover materials with desired and conflicting properties, like high dielectric constant and band gap simultaneously. The method not only enhances the generative capabilities but also facilitates property-driven material retrieval from the pre-training data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in applying reinforcement learning (RL), specifically mimicking RLHF,  to fine-tune an existing crystal generative model (CrystalFormer) using MLIPs and property prediction models as reward signals. While RL has been applied to other generative models, its application to a *crystal* generative model with a focus on material stability and property optimization using discriminative models as rewards is a valuable contribution. The concept of unlocking property-driven material retrieval from the unsupervised pre-training data through RL fine-tuning is also novel.  However, other works apply RL for materials synthesis optimization and the combination of generative and discrimintive models via active learning is well established, lowering this paper's relative novelty. The use of a specific MLIP (Orb model) is also a limitation since the method should work in principle with any MLIP; if there are limitations that only make it applicable to certain classes of MLIPs, this would further limit the overall novelty.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem in materials science: efficiently discovering novel materials with targeted properties. By integrating generative and discriminative models through RL, it offers a potentially powerful approach for accelerating materials discovery. The demonstrated improvements in stability and the ability to generate materials with conflicting properties highlight the method's practical relevance. The open-sourcing of the code and trained models further enhances the paper's significance by facilitating adoption and further research. The practical implications of enabling property-guided material retrieval from existing datasets are also significant.  The demonstration on real, conflicting properties like band gap and dielectric constant is valuable. Furthermore, that the RL fine-tuning also improves structure validity is important. However, the performance gain needs to be further scrutinized and benchmarked against alternative approaches. The results are also presented without full DFT verification, reducing their impact somewhat. Although claimed to reduce reliance on training labels, it should be kept in mind that the MLIP or property predictor has to be already trained on labeled datasets.

**Strengths:**

*   Clear problem definition and well-motivated approach.
*   Effective integration of generative and discriminative models via RL.
*   Demonstrated improvements in material stability and property optimization.
*   Code and model availability, promoting reproducibility and further research.
*  Includes tests for generated material structures that are novel, unique and stable.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Reliance on the *specific* Orb MLIP as a reward model without robustly demonstrating generalizability across several MLIPs.  (The paper says any MLIP can be used but doesn't test it robustly.)
*   Lack of extensive validation with DFT calculations to confirm the properties of the generated materials. (This makes the results less convincing; MLIPs are approximations of DFT, which themselves are approximations to the ground truth)
*  The gain on S.U.N. ratios could be further improved.
* Claims of "property-driven material retrieval" might be overstating the case since it doesn't appear like the algorithm *selects* existing materials from the pre-training set, but rather biases the generative model to be similar to ones in the dataset.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses outlined above, the paper merits a score of **7**. The application of RL fine-tuning to crystal generative models using MLIPs as rewards is a valuable and relatively novel approach that addresses a significant problem in materials discovery. The demonstrated improvements in stability and property optimization and the accessibility of the code are positive attributes. However, the limitations in generalizability across MLIPs, lack of full DFT validation, and the modest performance gain (compared to alternatives) reduce the score. The approach, while promising, requires more rigorous validation and broader applicability before it can be considered a truly exceptional contribution.
