### We Need Improved Data Curation and Attribution in AI for Scientific Discovery
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02486v1)
- **Authors**: Mara Graziani, Antonio Foncubierta, Dimitrios Christofidellis, Irina Espejo-Morales, Malina Molnar, Marvin Alberts, Matteo Manica, Jannis Born
- **Abstract**: As the interplay between human-generated and synthetic data evolves, new challenges arise in scientific discovery concerning the integrity of the data and the stability of the models. In this work, we examine the role of synthetic data as opposed to that of real experimental data for scientific research. Our analyses indicate that nearly three-quarters of experimental datasets available on open-access platforms have relatively low adoption rates, opening new opportunities to enhance their discoverability and usability by automated methods. Additionally, we observe an increasing difficulty in distinguishing synthetic from real experimental data. We propose supplementing ongoing efforts in automating synthetic data detection by increasing the focus on watermarking real experimental data, thereby strengthening data traceability and integrity. Our estimates suggest that watermarking even less than half of the real world data generated annually could help sustain model robustness, while promoting a balanced integration of synthetic and human-generated content.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, a critical evaluation, and a novelty/significance score for the provided paper.

**Summary:**

The paper "We Need Improved Data Curation and Attribution in AI for Scientific Discovery" investigates the growing role of synthetic data in scientific research and the challenges it poses to data integrity and model stability.  The authors analyze data sharing platforms like Hugging Face and Zenodo, finding a significant increase in synthetic data uploads, but inadequate documentation and tagging. They observe a concerning trend of underutilization of real-world experimental datasets.  The paper proposes a two-pronged approach: 1) automating dataset curation using agentic workflows and 2) enhancing the traceability of real experimental data through watermarking. They present evidence for performance degradation when models are trained on mixtures of real and synthetic data and suggest that even a small fraction of watermarked real data could significantly improve model robustness and encourage a more balanced integration of synthetic and human-generated content. The paper advocates for shifting focus to improving the curation and traceability of human data, which consequently will enhance the reliability and robustness of the models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Relevant Problem:** The paper tackles a timely and important issue: the increasing use of synthetic data and its impact on the reliability and trustworthiness of AI models in scientific discovery. As the field relies more on synthetic data, this analysis provides a solid early warning on risks.
    *   **Empirical Analysis:** The authors present data from real-world platforms (Hugging Face, Zenodo) to support their claims, lending credibility to their observations about synthetic data uploads, dataset adoption rates, and changes in linguistic patterns in scientific abstracts.
    *   **Clear Proposals:** The proposed solutions (automated curation, watermarking of real data) are concrete and actionable, providing a starting point for addressing the identified challenges. Watermarking is a well known strategy that needs to be revisited considering the recent advent of synthetic data.
    *   **Integration of Ideas:** It provides a compelling analogy between challenges in handling synthetic data and the persistence of outdated or retracted research.
    *   **Good writing style.**

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Quantification of Synthetic Data:** The paper acknowledges that their estimates of synthetic data prevalence are likely underestimates due to inconsistent tagging. This significantly weakens the empirical basis of their conclusions. Further exploration of this limitation is needed.
    *   **Lack of a Concrete Implementation:** While the proposals are clear, the paper lacks a detailed description of how automated curation or watermarking would be implemented in practice. The technical feasibility and cost-effectiveness of these solutions are not fully addressed. There is no experimental implementation to evaluate these results.
    *   **Oversimplified Model:** The watermarking simulation is based on simplified assumptions. The R^2 of .59 value used for prediction is low. The authors admit the value is not highly accurate, so it is difficult to conclude how much progress can be achieved with minimal effort.
    *   **Limited Novelty:** The two-pronged approach is not particularly novel.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    While the *problem* being addressed is highly relevant and growing in significance, the proposed solutions themselves lack significant novelty. Automated curation is an ongoing effort in many domains, and watermarking, while potentially valuable, has been discussed extensively in the context of synthetic data detection. The paper's contribution lies primarily in highlighting the need to shift focus to *real* data attribution and the practical implications within the context of scientific discovery. The analysis of data trends is valuable, but its impact is somewhat limited by the challenges in accurately quantifying synthetic data.

    The potential impact of the paper is that it could influence the direction of research in data curation and provenance tracking within the scientific community. It is a warning message to reinforce good practices.

**Score: 6/10**

**Justification:**

The paper earns a score of 6/10 because it effectively highlights an important, growing problem with good empirical support, even though there are limitations. The proposals for automation of curation and watermarking data have been identified as important areas to improve on. However, the relatively limited novelty of the proposed solutions and the lack of a concrete implementation plan prevent it from achieving a higher score. The significance of the paper lies in its potential to raise awareness within the scientific community and to shift focus towards real data attribution as a complementary strategy to synthetic data detection. Addressing the implementation challenges and refining the quantification of synthetic data would significantly enhance the paper's impact.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Semiconductor Wafer Map Defect Classification with Tiny Vision Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02494v1)
- **Authors**: Faisal Mohammad, Duksan Ryu
- **Abstract**: Semiconductor wafer defect classification is critical for ensuring high precision and yield in manufacturing. Traditional CNN-based models often struggle with class imbalances and recognition of the multiple overlapping defect types in wafer maps. To address these challenges, we propose ViT-Tiny, a lightweight Vision Transformer (ViT) framework optimized for wafer defect classification. Trained on the WM-38k dataset. ViT-Tiny outperforms its ViT-Base counterpart and state-of-the-art (SOTA) models, such as MSF-Trans and CNN-based architectures. Through extensive ablation studies, we determine that a patch size of 16 provides optimal performance. ViT-Tiny achieves an F1-score of 98.4%, surpassing MSF-Trans by 2.94% in four-defect classification, improving recall by 2.86% in two-defect classification, and increasing precision by 3.13% in three-defect classification. Additionally, it demonstrates enhanced robustness under limited labeled data conditions, making it a computationally efficient and reliable solution for real-world semiconductor defect detection.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, a critical evaluation, and a score for the paper based on the text provided.

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a lightweight Vision Transformer (ViT) framework, named ViT-Tiny, for semiconductor wafer map defect classification.  It argues that traditional CNN-based models struggle with class imbalances and recognition of multiple overlapping defect types. ViT-Tiny is trained on the WM-38k dataset and is claimed to outperform its ViT-Base counterpart and state-of-the-art (SOTA) models like MSF-Trans and CNN-based architectures in terms of F1-score, recall, and precision.  The paper also claims ViT-Tiny demonstrates enhanced robustness under limited labeled data conditions, making it a computationally efficient and reliable solution for real-world semiconductor defect detection.  Ablation studies are presented to show that a patch size of 16 provides optimal performance. The paper emphasizes ViT-Tiny's balance between accuracy, computational efficiency, and generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the *application* of ViT models to semiconductor wafer defect classification isn't entirely novel (as the paper itself cites related work), the focus on a *lightweight* ViT (ViT-Tiny) variant *specifically optimized* for this task, with a clear emphasis on real-time industrial deployment, has incremental novelty. However, ViT-Tiny itself is not a novel architecture, it is an existing model.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance lies in the claims of improved performance *and* reduced computational complexity compared to larger ViT models and other SOTA methods. If ViT-Tiny can truly achieve comparable or better accuracy with significantly fewer resources, this could have practical implications for real-world semiconductor manufacturing, where speed and cost are crucial.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Focus on Efficiency:** The clear focus on a lightweight model (ViT-Tiny) and its suitability for resource-constrained environments is a strength. The semiconductor industry is sensitive to computational costs.

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents results comparing ViT-Tiny to several baselines, including CNN-based and Transformer-based architectures. It considers both single and mixed-defect types.

    *   **Ablation Study:** The inclusion of an ablation study (varying patch size) adds some rigor to the analysis.

    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges of class imbalance and the complexity of defect patterns in wafer map classification.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Justification for ViT-Tiny choice:** The paper describes Vit-Tiny, but doesn't provide a thorough explanation *why* this is the best choice.
    *   **Dataset Specificity:** A major weakness is the reliance on the WM-38k dataset. The paper acknowledges this as a threat to external validity. Generalization to other datasets and industrial settings needs further validation. It does not make any effort to evaluate on any other dataset.

    *   **Lack of Detail on Training:** While hyperparameters are mentioned, the paper lacks some detail on the training process itself.

    *   **Limited discussion of SOTA comparison :** There is little discussion on how the SOTA models were implemented and retrained to ensure comparability.
    *   **Novelty: ** The approach relies on ViT-Tiny pre-trained weights from ImageNet. So, novelty is not in terms of training from scratch but on fine-tuning on the specific data set.
    *   **Model selection is not justified.** The approach does not justify why ViT-Tiny is selected for the study, out of multiple candidate computer vision models.
*   **Potential Influence:** If the results hold up in more diverse settings, the paper could influence the adoption of lightweight Transformer-based models for semiconductor defect detection. It provides a potential path towards more efficient and scalable defect detection systems.
**Rigorous Rationale:**

The assigned score is based on the balance between the paper's focus on a practical problem and potential solution versus its limitations in generalizability and lack of more detailed justification. The claimed improvements in accuracy and efficiency are promising, but the reliance on a single dataset and the lack of thorough discussion of the ViT-Tiny architecture selection prevent a higher score. The work has incremental novelty in the application domain and some practical potential, but lacks the rigor and breadth necessary for a truly high-impact contribution.

**Score: 6/10**

### Inference-Time Scaling for Generalist Reward Modeling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02495v1)
- **Authors**: Zijun Liu, Peiyi Wang, Runxin Xu, Shirong Ma, Chong Ruan, Peng Li, Yang Liu, Yu Wu
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has been widely adopted in post-training for large language models (LLMs) at scale. Recently, the incentivization of reasoning capabilities in LLMs from RL indicates that $\textit{proper learning methods could enable effective inference-time scalability}$. A key challenge of RL is to obtain accurate reward signals for LLMs in various domains beyond verifiable questions or artificial rules. In this work, we investigate how to improve reward modeling (RM) with more inference compute for general queries, i.e. the $\textbf{inference-time scalability of generalist RM}$, and further, how to improve the effectiveness of performance-compute scaling with proper learning methods. For the RM approach, we adopt pointwise generative reward modeling (GRM) to enable flexibility for different input types and potential for inference-time scaling. For the learning method, we propose Self-Principled Critique Tuning (SPCT) to foster scalable reward generation behaviors in GRMs through online RL, to generate principles adaptively and critiques accurately, resulting in $\textbf{DeepSeek-GRM}$ models. Furthermore, for effective inference-time scaling, we use parallel sampling to expand compute usage, and introduce a meta RM to guide voting process for better scaling performance. Empirically, we show that SPCT significantly improves the quality and scalability of GRMs, outperforming existing methods and models in various RM benchmarks without severe biases, and could achieve better performance compared to training-time scaling. DeepSeek-GRM still meets challenges in some tasks, which we believe can be addressed by future efforts in generalist reward systems. The models will be released and open-sourced.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Inference-Time Scaling for Generalist Reward Modeling":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of creating reward models (RMs) for large language models (LLMs) that can generalize well across various domains and scale effectively during inference.  The authors argue that obtaining accurate reward signals for LLMs in general scenarios, beyond simple verifiable tasks, is a crucial problem. They propose a novel learning method called Self-Principled Critique Tuning (SPCT) for pointwise generative reward modeling (GRM). SPCT aims to foster scalable reward generation behaviors through online RL, adaptively generating principles and critiques for the GRMs, resulting in DeepSeek-GRM models. The authors also explore inference-time scaling by using parallel sampling and a meta RM to guide the voting process for better performance. The experimental results show that SPCT improves the quality and scalability of GRMs, outperforming existing methods in several RM benchmarks, and sometimes outperforming training-time scaling in performance. The DeepSeek-GRM models and related code are intended to be released publicly.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel components:
    *   **Self-Principled Critique Tuning (SPCT):** This is the core contribution and seems like a promising approach to improve the reward modeling by learning to posit and critique principles instead of being limited to predefined or rule-based ones. Learning the critiques adaptively based on the input query and responses seems novel and can likely improve outcome rewards in general domains.
    *   **Meta RM for guided voting:** The use of a meta RM to refine the output of the base GRM through voting provides another potential mechanism to address biases in the GRM output.
    *   **Inference-time Scaling Approach:** Applying parallel sampling and a meta RM in this context is an important step towards making generalist RMs practical.

*   **Significance:** Reward modeling is a crucial aspect of aligning LLMs with human values and goals. A reliable and scalable reward system significantly impacts the overall performance of LLMs across various applications. The proposed DeepSeek-GRM can be a significant advance, provided it indeed enhances performance and broadens applications beyond specialized tasks. Furthermore, the proposed approach, which generates principles from within the model itself, has interesting implications for the potential of improving alignment via LLMs, rather than external criteria.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors evaluated their method on several RM benchmarks and showed that it outperforms existing methods in most cases. The comprehensive evaluation provides strong evidence of the effectiveness of the proposed method.
    *   **Practical Focus:** The paper highlights the need for RMs to be both generalizable and scalable in inference. The solutions proposed address these practical challenges.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results demonstrate the potential of SPCT to improve the quality and scalability of GRMs. The paper also analyzes how larger models, such as the DeepSeek-V3 (671B parameters), perform compared to the DeepSeek-GRM 27B.
    *   **Reproducibility:**  The authors have committed to releasing models and code, which is crucial for reproducibility and further research in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Efficiency:** The paper notes that the GRMs are currently less efficient than scalar RMs, which could be a limitation for some applications. This issue should be addressed to enable broader usage in the field.
    *   **Limited Scope:** While the paper shows improvement in multiple benchmarks, it also states that their approach faces challenges in some tasks, indicating there is still room for improvement in generalizability. In addition, how exactly the challenges for specific tasks arise should be more deeply analyzed and addressed in the next stage.
    *   **Unclear Implementation Details:** The authors claim they set G=4 to increase efficiency and performance but the impact is unknown, and it may be better to show the performance by setting G to different values to demonstrate the effect of the hyperparameter.
    *   **Lack of novelty in method:** While the approach has novelty, many parts of the model include existing architectures or previously published methods, and the paper doesn't properly acknowledge the overlap with the prior work, such as by citing key related methods that could add value and clarity to the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the paper's claims hold and the released models are as effective as described, DeepSeek-GRM could become a popular choice for reward modeling and influence how LLMs are aligned in the future. Further research in the efficiency of generative rewards would be encouraged.
* **Additional Comments:**
The current version of the paper has some issues in formatting and citation - it is essential to address these shortcomings before final release of the paper.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**
The paper presents novel and promising methods (SPCT, meta RM) for improving generalist reward modeling and scaling it during inference. The empirical results support these claims, but weaknesses in efficiency and scope reduce the score. Though the paper would benefit from more detailed analysis of its limitations and implementation challenges, the potential impact of the proposed approach warrants this high score. If the claims in the paper hold true, then the proposed method provides significant benefits in improving reward quality for large language models.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ZClip: Adaptive Spike Mitigation for LLM Pre-Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02507v1)
- **Authors**: Abhay Kumar, Louis Owen, Nilabhra Roy Chowdhury, Fabian Güra
- **Abstract**: Training large language models (LLMs) presents numerous challenges, including gradient instability and loss spikes. These phenomena can lead to catastrophic divergence, requiring costly checkpoint restoration and data batch skipping. Traditional gradient clipping techniques, such as constant or norm-based methods, fail to address these issues effectively due to their reliance on fixed thresholds or heuristics, leading to inefficient learning and requiring frequent manual intervention. In this work, we propose ZClip, an adaptive gradient clipping algorithm that dynamically adjusts the clipping threshold based on statistical properties of gradient norms over time. Unlike prior reactive strategies, ZClip proactively adapts to training dynamics without making any prior assumptions on the scale and the temporal evolution of gradient norms. At its core, it leverages z-score-based anomaly detection to identify and mitigate large gradient spikes, preventing malignant loss spikes while not interfering with convergence otherwise. Our code is available at: https://github.com/bluorion-com/ZClip.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the ZClip paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ZClip, an adaptive gradient clipping algorithm designed to mitigate loss spikes during the pre-training of large language models (LLMs).  ZClip dynamically adjusts the clipping threshold based on the z-score of gradient norms, calculated using exponential moving averages (EMA) of the mean and variance of gradient norms. By proactively adapting to training dynamics, ZClip aims to prevent catastrophic divergence, reduce the need for manual intervention (checkpoint restarts and batch skipping), and enable faster convergence. The paper presents experimental results on a 1B LLaMA model, demonstrating that ZClip can stabilize training at higher learning rates, eliminate loss spikes, and improve downstream task performance compared to fixed-threshold gradient clipping and percentile-based clipping (AutoClip).

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of adaptive gradient clipping isn't entirely new (AutoClip being a prime example). However, the paper's novelty lies in the specific implementation using z-scores derived from EMA statistics for anomaly detection and dynamic clipping. The use of EMA for maintaining a lightweight history and adapting to the evolving training dynamics is a practical contribution. The reciprocal clipping function also offers a nuanced approach to scaling gradient adjustments based on the severity of the anomaly. The warm-up and spike-handling statistics updates are a nice touch to ensure that the statistics remain representative of the stable training regime.

**Significance:** Loss spikes are a major practical challenge in LLM pre-training, consuming time and resources.  ZClip provides a practical and relatively easy-to-implement solution. The demonstrated improvement in convergence speed and training stability, especially at aggressive learning rates, has significant potential for reducing the overall cost of LLM pre-training.  The comparison against existing methods (fixed-threshold clipping and AutoClip) highlights ZClip's advantages. The thorough analysis of hyperparameters and their impact on training adds value.

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical and easy to implement:**  ZClip can be readily implemented with existing training pipelines. The authors provide code.
*   **Effective mitigation of loss spikes:**  The experiments show a consistent reduction or elimination of loss spikes in various training scenarios.
*   **Improved convergence:**  ZClip enables stable training at higher learning rates, leading to faster convergence and reduced training costs.
*   **Adaptability:** The z-score based approach adapts to changing training dynamics without requiring manual tuning of the clipping threshold.
*   **Lightweight Implementation:** EMA is computationally cheaper than approaches such as AutoClip, which requires full storage of the past gradients.
*   **Comprehensive experimental evaluation:** The paper includes a thorough analysis of hyperparameters and their impact.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited scale of experiments:**  The primary experiments are conducted on a 1B LLaMA model. While the results are promising, the performance and benefits of ZClip at larger scales (7B, 70B, or larger) remain to be demonstrated. The effectiveness might be less pronounced at larger scales.
*   **Normality assumption:** The z-score method relies on the assumption that gradient norms approximately follow a normal distribution over short time windows. While the authors present some empirical validation, deviations from normality could affect the accuracy of anomaly detection.
*   **Lack of theoretical analysis:** While the paper provides empirical evidence of ZClip's effectiveness, it lacks a formal theoretical analysis to explain its behavior and convergence properties.
*   **Dependency on EMA parameters:** Despite the claim of adaptability, the performance is sensitive to the parameters of the EMA filter, as shown by the analysis on alpha.
*  **Lack of ablations on the statistical updates when a spike is detected** The effect of updating statistics with the clipped gradient is unclear.

**Potential Influence:** ZClip has the potential to become a standard technique for gradient clipping in LLM pre-training.  Its ease of implementation and effectiveness in stabilizing training could encourage wider adoption within the community.  The paper could also inspire further research into adaptive gradient clipping methods that leverage statistical properties of gradients to improve training stability and efficiency.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper makes a solid practical contribution to the field of LLM training by introducing a new, adaptive gradient clipping algorithm (ZClip). The strengths of ZClip lie in its effectiveness, ease of implementation, and adaptability to changing training dynamics, as demonstrated in the experiments.  While the core idea of adaptive clipping isn't entirely novel, the z-score and EMA based implementation and the spike-handling update strategy add a novel angle. The thorough experimental evaluation and hyperparameter analysis support the claims made in the paper. The primary limitation is the scale of the experiments, which are limited to a 1B model, making it hard to extrapolate to larger scale models. The lack of ablations on the statistical updates, and the dependence on parameters for the EMA filter also limit the potential of the approach.


- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### APHQ-ViT: Post-Training Quantization with Average Perturbation Hessian Based Reconstruction for Vision Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02508v1)
- **Authors**: Zhuguanyu Wu, Jiayi Zhang, Jiaxin Chen, Jinyang Guo, Di Huang, Yunhong Wang
- **Abstract**: Vision Transformers (ViTs) have become one of the most commonly used backbones for vision tasks. Despite their remarkable performance, they often suffer significant accuracy drops when quantized for practical deployment, particularly by post-training quantization (PTQ) under ultra-low bits. Recently, reconstruction-based PTQ methods have shown promising performance in quantizing Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs). However, they fail when applied to ViTs, primarily due to the inaccurate estimation of output importance and the substantial accuracy degradation in quantizing post-GELU activations. To address these issues, we propose \textbf{APHQ-ViT}, a novel PTQ approach based on importance estimation with Average Perturbation Hessian (APH). Specifically, we first thoroughly analyze the current approximation approaches with Hessian loss, and propose an improved average perturbation Hessian loss. To deal with the quantization of the post-GELU activations, we design an MLP Reconstruction (MR) method by replacing the GELU function in MLP with ReLU and reconstructing it by the APH loss on a small unlabeled calibration set. Extensive experiments demonstrate that APHQ-ViT using linear quantizers outperforms existing PTQ methods by substantial margins in 3-bit and 4-bit across different vision tasks. The source code is available at https://github.com/GoatWu/APHQ-ViT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the APHQ-ViT paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces APHQ-ViT, a novel post-training quantization (PTQ) method specifically designed for Vision Transformers (ViTs).  It addresses two key limitations of existing PTQ approaches when applied to ViTs: inaccurate estimation of output importance and performance degradation when quantizing activations after GELU. To tackle these issues, APHQ-ViT proposes: (1) An improved "Average Perturbation Hessian" (APH) loss for more precise importance estimation during block reconstruction, and (2) An "MLP Reconstruction" (MR) method that replaces the GELU activation in MLPs with ReLU, reconstructing the MLP to reduce activation range and alleviate imbalanced activation distributions.  Experiments demonstrate that APHQ-ViT, using linear quantizers, achieves significantly better performance than existing PTQ methods, especially at ultra-low bitwidths (3-bit and 4-bit).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces two key innovations: the APH loss and the MLP Reconstruction technique. The APH loss is presented as an improvement over existing Hessian-based approximations, designed to better capture the importance of different outputs within ViTs. The MLP Reconstruction is a clever way to address the challenges posed by quantizing post-GELU activations, particularly the imbalanced distribution. The idea of replacing GELU with ReLU and then reconstructing is novel and directly addresses the issues observed in the activations.

*   **Significance:** Model quantization is crucial for deploying deep learning models on resource-constrained devices. The paper tackles a real and important problem: the difficulty of quantizing ViTs effectively.  The empirical results show substantial gains over existing methods, especially at ultra-low bitwidths, making the approach potentially very valuable for real-world applications where extreme compression is needed. However, the gains at higher bitwidths, while positive, might be less dramatic and impactful.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clearly Identified Problem:**  The paper clearly articulates the challenges of quantizing ViTs, particularly the post-GELU activation issue and the inaccurate importance estimation.
    *   **Well-Motivated Solutions:** The proposed APH loss and MLP Reconstruction are well-motivated by the analysis of the problems.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating significant improvements over state-of-the-art PTQ methods, especially at low bitwidths. Extensive experiments are conducted across multiple ViT architectures and vision tasks (image classification, object detection, instance segmentation)
    *   **Thorough Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies are well-designed and provide insights into the contribution of each component (APH loss and MLP Reconstruction).
    *   **Analysis of inference efficiency on MR:** The paper showed the MLP Reconstruction method not only promotes quantization accuracy but also accelerates inference

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Independence Assumptions in APH:** The derivation of the APH loss relies on some independence assumptions that might not always hold perfectly in practice. The impact of these assumptions should be further explored.
    *   **Limited Quantizer Types:** The evaluation focuses primarily on linear quantizers. While the results are impressive, it would be beneficial to see how APHQ-ViT performs with other quantizer types (e.g., logarithmic, mixed-precision).
    *   **Limited analysis on datasets:** Although, the models have been validated for multiple tasks, only COCO and ImageNet datasets have been used.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the method can be generalized to other transformer-based models and tasks, and if the gains at ultra-low bitwidths are maintained in real-world deployments, it could have a significant impact on the deployment of ViTs in resource-constrained environments.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the novel approach to addressing specific challenges in ViT quantization, the compelling empirical results, particularly at ultra-low bitwidths, and the thorough ablation studies, the paper represents a significant contribution to the field. While the independence assumptions and the limited analysis of alternative quantizers represent weaknesses, the strengths outweigh the limitations. Therefore, the paper warrants a high score.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### MultiNeRF: Multiple Watermark Embedding for Neural Radiance Fields
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02517v1)
- **Authors**: Yash Kulthe, Andrew Gilbert, John Collomosse
- **Abstract**: We present MultiNeRF, a 3D watermarking method that embeds multiple uniquely keyed watermarks within images rendered by a single Neural Radiance Field (NeRF) model, whilst maintaining high visual quality. Our approach extends the TensoRF NeRF model by incorporating a dedicated watermark grid alongside the existing geometry and appearance grids. This extension ensures higher watermark capacity without entangling watermark signals with scene content. We propose a FiLM-based conditional modulation mechanism that dynamically activates watermarks based on input identifiers, allowing multiple independent watermarks to be embedded and extracted without requiring model retraining. MultiNeRF is validated on the NeRF-Synthetic and LLFF datasets, with statistically significant improvements in robust capacity without compromising rendering quality. By generalizing single-watermark NeRF methods into a flexible multi-watermarking framework, MultiNeRF provides a scalable solution for 3D content. attribution.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MultiNeRF, a novel approach to embedding multiple, uniquely keyed watermarks within Neural Radiance Field (NeRF) models. It extends the TensoRF NeRF model by adding a dedicated watermark grid and a FiLM-based conditional modulation mechanism. This allows for embedding and extracting multiple independent watermarks without retraining the model. The paper demonstrates that MultiNeRF achieves improved robust capacity without significantly compromising rendering quality on standard NeRF datasets. By providing a multi-watermarking framework, the paper aims to offer a scalable solution for 3D content attribution and IP protection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in extending existing single-watermark NeRF methods to handle multiple, conditionally activated watermarks. Adding a separate watermark grid is a good way to prevent the watermark from interfering with the scene rendering. The use of FiLM-based modulation for conditional watermark activation is innovative in the NeRF context.

*   **Significance:** Addressing the challenge of IP protection for NeRF models is crucial as their usage grows. The ability to embed multiple watermarks significantly enhances the usefulness of this approach, as it allows different stakeholders (e.g., co-creators, licensees) to assert their rights. The paper tackles a practically relevant problem with a well-designed solution, and the provided results show a significant improvement compared to the watermarking of NeRF model methods limited to encoding a single watermark.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the need for watermarking in NeRF models and the limitations of existing single-watermark methods.
    *   **Technical Soundness:** The proposed architecture, incorporating the watermark grid and FiLM modulation, is technically solid and justified.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of MultiNeRF in terms of watermark capacity, rendering quality, and robustness against attacks on standard datasets.
    *   **Scalability:** The framework offers a potential solution for real-world collaborative and commercial settings where multiple stakeholders need to assert ownership.
    *   Adding a separate watermark grid ensures higher watermark capacity without entangling watermark signals with scene content.
    *   The authors augment training with differentiable noise sources to ensure robustness.
    *   The paper is clearly written and well-organized.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Attack Evaluation:** While the paper tests robustness, a more comprehensive evaluation against a wider range of attacks (e.g., geometric distortions, adversarial attacks tailored to NeRFs) would strengthen the robustness claim.
    *   **Model Size Overhead:** The addition of the watermark grid increases the model size, which could be a concern for deployment in memory-constrained environments. The paper acknowledges a 12% overhead and needs further address the trade-off between watermark capacity and model size.
    *   **User Study Limitation:** While the user study assesses watermark artifact perception, a more extensive study with a larger and more diverse group of participants is desired.
    *   Lack of analysis as to how the multiple watermarks are encoded within the dedicated watermark grid.

*   **Potential Influence:** MultiNeRF has the potential to significantly influence the field of NeRFs by paving the way for more robust and practical IP protection mechanisms. It can inspire future research on developing more efficient and secure watermarking techniques for 3D generative models.

**Justification:**

MultiNeRF makes a significant contribution by addressing the limitations of existing NeRF watermarking methods. The capability of embedding multiple watermarks is a substantial advancement that opens up new possibilities for content attribution and licensing. The technical approach is well-designed, and the results are promising. The weaknesses, such as the limited attack evaluation and model size overhead, are acknowledged and represent areas for future improvement. I assign it a score of 8, given its novelty and potential impact.

**Score: 8**
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### UNDO: Understanding Distillation as Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02521v1)
- **Authors**: Kushal Jain, Piyushi Goyal, Kumar Shridhar
- **Abstract**: Knowledge distillation has emerged as an effective strategy for compressing large language models' (LLMs) knowledge into smaller, more efficient student models. However, standard one-shot distillation methods often produce suboptimal results due to a mismatch between teacher-generated rationales and the student's specific learning requirements. In this paper, we introduce the UNDO: UNderstanding Distillation as Optimization framework, designed to bridge this gap by iteratively identifying the student's errors and prompting the teacher to refine its explanations accordingly. Each iteration directly targets the student's learning deficiencies, motivating the teacher to provide tailored and enhanced rationales that specifically address these weaknesses. Empirical evaluations on various challenging mathematical and commonsense reasoning tasks demonstrate that our iterative distillation method, UNDO, significantly outperforms standard one-step distillation methods, achieving performance gains of up to 20%. Additionally, we show that teacher-generated data refined through our iterative process remains effective even when applied to different student models, underscoring the broad applicability of our approach. Our work fundamentally reframes knowledge distillation as an iterative teacher-student interaction, effectively leveraging dynamic refinement by the teacher for better knowledge distillation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "UNDO: UNderstanding Distillation as Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UNDO, a novel iterative knowledge distillation framework designed to improve student models' performance by addressing the mismatch between teacher-generated rationales and student learning needs.  Unlike standard one-shot distillation, UNDO iteratively identifies student errors, prompts the teacher model to generate refined, targeted explanations, and fine-tunes the student model based on these refined rationales. The framework aims to personalize training data to the student's specific needs. Experimental results on mathematical and commonsense reasoning tasks demonstrate that UNDO significantly outperforms standard distillation methods. The authors also show that the refined teacher-generated data is effective across different student models, highlighting the method's robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of iteratively refining teacher rationales based on student performance is novel. While prior work has explored iterative distillation and self-training, UNDO's explicit focus on creating a dynamic feedback loop between teacher and student, with the teacher adapting *its explanations* to address specific student weaknesses, represents a distinctive contribution. The connection to educational concepts like scaffolding and formative assessment provides a sound conceptual basis. This moves beyond simply transferring knowledge from teacher to student to creating a learning *process*.
*   **Significance:** The reported performance gains (up to 20%) are significant, especially on challenging reasoning tasks. The finding that refined teacher data is effective across different student models enhances the practical value of the method. Furthermore, the demonstration of improved performance on out-of-domain tasks suggests that UNDO leads to more robust and generalizable student models, a crucial aspect of knowledge distillation. However, the computational overhead of running LLM-based teachers multiple times for data generation and evaluation should be noted as a practical limitation.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-motivated approach.
    *   Strong empirical results on challenging tasks.
    *   The method is applicable across different student models.
    *   Improved performance on out-of-domain tasks.
    *   The connection to educational theory strengthens the motivation.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The computational cost, particularly the reliance on LLMs for iterative data generation and evaluation, is a potential barrier to wider adoption. The paper mentions significant GPU hours, a potential resource bottleneck.
    *   The reliance on the teacher model's ability to provide effective and targeted rationales. If the teacher consistently fails to address student weaknesses, the method could stagnate. More analysis on when and how the teacher prompt works would be very helpful.
    *   The study focuses primarily on mathematical and common sense reasoning. Assessing its effectiveness on other domains would be beneficial.
    *   More detailed ablation studies could be insightful, particularly regarding the impact of different components of the teacher prompt and the number of iterations.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of knowledge distillation by shifting the focus from one-shot transfer to iterative, personalized learning. It could inspire new research directions on adaptive distillation methods, teacher-student interaction strategies, and the role of pedagogical principles in model compression.
    *   However, the practical utility would depend on future work focusing on efficient teacher prompting strategies and data generation.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper presents a genuinely novel and impactful approach to knowledge distillation by incorporating an iterative feedback loop that personalizes the teacher's rationales to address the student's specific learning gaps. The strong empirical results across multiple tasks, along with the demonstration of out-of-domain generalization, supports the significance of the contribution. However, the method's computational cost and dependence on the teacher's abilities are important limitations. The paper moves beyond standard methods in knowledge distillation and can impact a shift in the field that may allow for smaller, more useful models with personalized learning to reach higher levels of accuracy.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Charm: The Missing Piece in ViT fine-tuning for Image Aesthetic Assessment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02522v1)
- **Authors**: Fatemeh Behrad, Tinne Tuytelaars, Johan Wagemans
- **Abstract**: The capacity of Vision transformers (ViTs) to handle variable-sized inputs is often constrained by computational complexity and batch processing limitations. Consequently, ViTs are typically trained on small, fixed-size images obtained through downscaling or cropping. While reducing computational burden, these methods result in significant information loss, negatively affecting tasks like image aesthetic assessment. We introduce Charm, a novel tokenization approach that preserves Composition, High-resolution, Aspect Ratio, and Multi-scale information simultaneously. Charm prioritizes high-resolution details in specific regions while downscaling others, enabling shorter fixed-size input sequences for ViTs while incorporating essential information. Charm is designed to be compatible with pre-trained ViTs and their learned positional embeddings. By providing multiscale input and introducing variety to input tokens, Charm improves ViT performance and generalizability for image aesthetic assessment. We avoid cropping or changing the aspect ratio to further preserve information. Extensive experiments demonstrate significant performance improvements on various image aesthetic and quality assessment datasets (up to 8.1 %) using a lightweight ViT backbone. Code and pre-trained models are available at https://github.com/FBehrad/Charm.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Charm: The Missing Piece in ViT fine-tuning for Image Aesthetic Assessment."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Charm, a novel tokenization approach for Vision Transformers (ViTs) that aims to improve image aesthetic assessment (IAA) and image quality assessment (IQA) performance.  Charm addresses the limitations of standard ViT preprocessing methods (downscaling or cropping) which can lead to information loss and distorted aesthetics. Charm works by preserving high-resolution details in specific regions of an image while downscaling others, resulting in shorter input sequences for ViTs and more effectively balancing computational cost and information preservation. Charm is designed to be compatible with pre-trained ViTs and their learned positional embeddings and also incorporates multi-scale input. The authors demonstrate significant performance improvements on various IAA and IQA datasets by fine-tuning a lightweight ViT backbone with Charm. Code and pre-trained models are stated as publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of selectively maintaining high-resolution regions within an image while downscaling others for ViT processing is novel in the *specific context of image aesthetic and quality assessment*.  While mixed-resolution and multi-scale tokenization methods exist, the paper argues that Charm addresses the specific requirements of IAA more effectively than previous approaches, particularly in preserving composition and aspect ratio. The key contribution is the carefully designed tokenization process optimized for aesthetic and quality assessment. However, the idea itself builds on the prior work by others that include mixed resolution images.

*   **Significance:** Image aesthetic and quality assessment has significant real-world applications in areas like photography, advertising, e-commerce, and social media. If Charm's performance improvements hold up under further scrutiny, the approach can have practical benefits in these domains. By enabling the use of lighter weight ViT models, this can also reduce computation burden for those applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results across multiple datasets, demonstrating significant performance improvements with Charm compared to standard ViT fine-tuning and other related methods (including a comparison to adding just padding or the Muller resizer). The detailed ablation studies provide good support for the design choices.
    *   **Compatibility:**  The method is designed to be compatible with existing pre-trained ViTs and their learned positional embeddings, a significant advantage over approaches requiring extensive re-training.
    *   **Computational Efficiency:**  Charm effectively balances performance with computational cost, allowing the use of smaller ViT models while maintaining accuracy.
    *   **Clear Presentation:**  The paper is well-written and clearly explains the method and its benefits.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Exploration of Patch Selection:** The investigation of patch selection strategies could have been more thorough. While the "random in each epoch" strategy performs well, more exploration into advanced selection methods tailored to IAA or IQA could yield further improvements. More comparison between those methods in terms of time and complexity would be helpful.
    *   **Dependence on Downscaling Factor:** The method performance depends on the scaling factor *f*. More robust explanation or techniques on how to choose *f* for more general application is needed.
    *   **Limited Comparison with all SOTA Approaches:** While it compares Charm's performance with a diverse set of published SOTA techniques, there are more that could have been directly tested/compared.
    *  **Missing or Limited Code:** While the paper mentioned the code would be available at Github, it wasn't accessible at the time of review.
*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   **Improved IAA Models:**  Charm could become a standard preprocessing or tokenization technique for future IAA and IQA models based on ViTs.
    *   **Real-world Applications:**  The improved performance and efficiency of Charm could facilitate the deployment of IAA and IQA models in real-world applications with limited computational resources.
    *   **Future Research:** The paper opens new avenues for exploring selective attention and multi-scale processing in ViTs, specifically tailored for tasks where preserving image aesthetics and quality is crucial.
* **Reasons to Reduce the Score**
    *   The core idea, while novel for the task, borrows aspects of well-established techniques.
    *   Code availability needs to be improved.
    *   The performance with a few SOTA techniques could be missing.
    *   The dependency on the downscaling factor *f* may hinder general use cases.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** Charm presents a novel and effective approach for fine-tuning ViTs for IAA and IQA. The experimental results are compelling, and the design choices are well-justified. However, it is an incremental improvement on current existing methods. The limitations with the parameter *f* could be better explored or techniques offered on choosing *f* more generally. The inclusion of more state-of-the-art methods could have improved the argument. Nevertheless, the demonstrated performance gains with a light weight ViT backbone can have substantial and meaningful impact. If the claims are validated with future testing and implementations, this offers the potential to influence future research and practice in this field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### SelfMedHPM: Self Pre-training With Hard Patches Mining Masked Autoencoders For Medical Image Segmentation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02524v1)
- **Authors**: Yunhao Lv, Lingyu Chen, Jian Wang, Yangxi Li, Fang Chen
- **Abstract**: In recent years, deep learning methods such as convolutional neural network (CNN) and transformers have made significant progress in CT multi-organ segmentation. However, CT multi-organ segmentation methods based on masked image modeling (MIM) are very limited. There are already methods using MAE for CT multi-organ segmentation task, we believe that the existing methods do not identify the most difficult areas to reconstruct. To this end, we propose a MIM self-training framework with hard patches mining masked autoencoders for CT multi-organ segmentation tasks (selfMedHPM). The method performs ViT self-pretraining on the training set of the target data and introduces an auxiliary loss predictor, which first predicts the patch loss and determines the location of the next mask. SelfMedHPM implementation is better than various competitive methods in abdominal CT multi-organ segmentation and body CT multi-organ segmentation. We have validated the performance of our method on the Multi Atlas Labeling Beyond The Cranial Vault (BTCV) dataset for abdomen mult-organ segmentation and the SinoMed Whole Body (SMWB) dataset for body multi-organ segmentation tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "SELFMEDHPM: SELF PRE-TRAINING WITH HARD PATCHES MINING MASKED AUTOENCODERS FOR MEDICAL IMAGE SEGMENTATION" introduces a novel self-supervised pre-training method (SelfMedHPM) for medical image segmentation, specifically applied to CT scans.  The core idea is to improve Masked Autoencoder (MAE) training by focusing on the "hardest" patches to reconstruct. Instead of random masking, the method employs an auxiliary loss predictor to estimate the reconstruction loss for each patch.  Patches with high predicted loss are then preferentially masked. This encourages the model to learn more about difficult or discriminative areas (e.g., organ boundaries). The pre-trained model is then used as the encoder in a UNETR architecture for segmentation. Experiments on two datasets (BTCV and SMWB) demonstrate improved segmentation performance compared to existing MAE-based pre-training techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:**  The core idea of focusing on "hard" patches for masking in self-supervised medical image segmentation is a valuable contribution. It addresses a key limitation of standard MAE, which treats all patches equally. The introduction of the auxiliary loss predictor to estimate patch difficulty is clever.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The paper provides strong empirical evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed method. The performance gains on both BTCV and SMWB datasets, as well as improvements against comparable MAE baselines, are significant.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method. The illustration (Fig. 1) is helpful in visualizing the training process.
    *   **Ablation Study:**  The ablation study convincingly demonstrates the necessity of predicting reconstruction loss (Lpred) for improving segmentation performance, reinforcing the value of the "teacher-student" approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalization Discussion:** While the paper demonstrates strong performance on CT datasets of specific abdominal and full-body regions, there's insufficient discussion about the generalizability of SelfMedHPM to other imaging modalities (e.g., MRI, Ultrasound) or different anatomical regions with varying characteristics.  The "hard" patches may manifest differently in different contexts.
    *   **Complexity of Loss Prediction:** While the use of an auxiliary loss predictor is novel, the details provided regarding its loss function may need further investigation. It would have been helpful to compare performance against a more simple strategy of estimating loss without an auxiliary predictor such as directly training for estimating reconstruction error
    *   **Parameter Sensitivity:**  The algorithm has several hyperparameters (e.g., *a0*, *aT*, layer decay ratio, and DropPath probability). The paper lacks thorough exploration of how these parameters affect the model's performance.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** While the empirical results are strong, there's a relative absence of theoretical justification. It would be beneficial to explore the relationships between predicted loss and patch relevance or how this approach compares to attention mechanisms within transformers

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of self-supervised learning for medical image segmentation. The technique of focusing pre-training on hard patches is likely to inspire further research.
    *   The improved segmentation performance could have practical benefits in clinical applications.

**Overall Score:**

I would assign a score of **7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper introduces a novel and effective self-supervised learning method for medical image segmentation. The empirical results are compelling, and the ablation study supports the core idea. The method enhances masked autoencoder pre-training, addressing a key limitation. However, the paper could be strengthened by a more thorough discussion of generalizability, a detailed exploration of hyperparameter sensitivities, and a deeper theoretical analysis of the benefits of the approach. The method has great potential and a real-world impact on downstream segmentation.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 0/10

### A Sensorimotor Vision Transformer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02536v1)
- **Authors**: Konrad Gadzicki, Kerstin Schill, Christoph Zetzsche
- **Abstract**: This paper presents the Sensorimotor Transformer (SMT), a vision model inspired by human saccadic eye movements that prioritize high-saliency regions in visual input to enhance computational efficiency and reduce memory consumption. Unlike traditional models that process all image patches uniformly, SMT identifies and selects the most salient patches based on intrinsic two-dimensional (i2D) features, such as corners and occlusions, which are known to convey high-information content and align with human fixation patterns. The SMT architecture uses this biological principle to leverage vision transformers to process only the most informative patches, allowing for a substantial reduction in memory usage that scales with the sequence length of selected patches. This approach aligns with visual neuroscience findings, suggesting that the human visual system optimizes information gathering through selective, spatially dynamic focus. Experimental evaluations on Imagenet-1k demonstrate that SMT achieves competitive top-1 accuracy while significantly reducing memory consumption and computational complexity, particularly when a limited number of patches is used. This work introduces a saccade-like selection mechanism into transformer-based vision models, offering an efficient alternative for image analysis and providing new insights into biologically motivated architectures for resource-constrained applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "A Sensorimotor Vision Transformer":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Sensorimotor Transformer (SMT), a novel vision transformer architecture inspired by human saccadic eye movements.  Unlike standard ViTs that process all image patches uniformly, the SMT incorporates a saliency map based on intrinsic two-dimensional (i2D) features (corners, occlusions, etc.) to select only the most informative patches for processing. This selective attention mechanism aims to reduce memory consumption and computational complexity, aligning with the biological principle that the human visual system prioritizes high-saliency regions. The authors demonstrate that SMT achieves competitive accuracy on ImageNet-1k while significantly reducing memory usage, especially when processing a limited number of patches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of integrating saccade-inspired patch selection into a ViT is relatively novel. While attention mechanisms exist in transformers, this work explicitly mimics the biological process of selective foveal fixations based on intrinsic image features (i2D signals). The novelty lies in bridging sensorimotor principles from biological vision with transformer architectures. However, the individual components are not entirely novel. Saliency prediction based on i2D features has been studied before, and patch-based transformers are also established. The novel combination is the main contribution.
*   **Significance:** The potential significance of the work lies in its ability to address the computational bottlenecks of ViTs, particularly in resource-constrained environments. If the SMT can achieve comparable or even slightly lower accuracy than standard ViTs while offering substantial memory and computational savings, it could be a valuable architecture for mobile devices or edge computing applications. Furthermore, the work offers potentially valuable insights into biologically motivated architectures for resource-constrained applications. The research is consistent with the current trend of developing efficient and interpretable neural networks.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Biologically Inspired Design:** The motivation is strong, and the link to human visual processing is well-articulated.
    *   **Computational Efficiency:** The selective patch processing provides significant reduction in memory usage.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly explains the SMT architecture and the saliency prediction mechanism.
    *   **Quantitative Results:** The experiments demonstrate the trade-off between accuracy and memory usage.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Experimental Evaluation:** The experiments are primarily on ImageNet-1k. Further testing on different datasets (e.g., object detection, segmentation tasks) would strengthen the claim of general applicability.
    *   **Suboptimal Accuracy:** The reported accuracy of SMT is slightly lower than the standard ViT. The authors mention that parameters weren't optimized, but closing the accuracy gap with parameter tuning is crucial for practical adoption.
    *   **Saliency Prediction Simplification:** While using i2D features is biologically plausible, other saliency cues (e.g., higher-level semantic information) could further enhance performance. The current saliency mechanism might be too simplistic compared to the complexities of human visual attention.
    *   **Lack of comparison with other efficient ViTs**: The paper lacks direct comparisons against other efficient transformer variants. This makes it difficult to assess the performance benefits specific to the biologically inspired saliency-driven selection compared to other techniques used to improve performance of transformers.
    *   **Overemphasis on biological accuracy:** The paper could be more realistic and take the best components of biological vision. For example, biological vision involves many saccades, each providing a very brief snapshot of image content that the model does not seem to be leveraging.

*   **Potential Impact:**
    *   **Efficient Vision Models:** SMT provides a template for developing efficient vision models that reduce memory and computational demands.
    *   **Bio-inspired Architectures:** Can inspire further research in incorporating biological principles into deep learning architectures.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper has several strengths, most notably its clever adaptation of the saccade process into a vision transformer architecture to reduce model complexity. However, the slight accuracy drop compared to baseline ViTs and the lack of more comprehensive experimental validation prevents it from being a truly exceptional contribution at this stage. Lack of comparison to other efficient ViTs also weakens the claim that the proposed method offers advantages beyond that of other available methods.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### MAD: Makeup All-in-One with Cross-Domain Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02545v1)
- **Authors**: Bo-Kai Ruan, Hong-Han Shuai
- **Abstract**: Existing makeup techniques often require designing multiple models to handle different inputs and align features across domains for different makeup tasks, e.g., beauty filter, makeup transfer, and makeup removal, leading to increased complexity. Another limitation is the absence of text-guided makeup try-on, which is more user-friendly without needing reference images. In this study, we make the first attempt to use a single model for various makeup tasks. Specifically, we formulate different makeup tasks as cross-domain translations and leverage a cross-domain diffusion model to accomplish all tasks. Unlike existing methods that rely on separate encoder-decoder configurations or cycle-based mechanisms, we propose using different domain embeddings to facilitate domain control. This allows for seamless domain switching by merely changing embeddings with a single model, thereby reducing the reliance on additional modules for different tasks. Moreover, to support precise text-to-makeup applications, we introduce the MT-Text dataset by extending the MT dataset with textual annotations, advancing the practicality of makeup technologies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MAD (Makeup All-in-One), a framework that leverages a single cross-domain diffusion model for various makeup-related tasks, including beauty filters, makeup removal, text-guided makeup modification, and different types of makeup transfer.  The key idea is to formulate these tasks as domain translation problems, using learnable domain embeddings to control the translation process within the diffusion model. The framework also incorporates component asynchronous masking (CAM) for improved makeup application in smaller areas like lips and eyebrows, and introduces MT-Text, a new dataset extending the existing MT dataset with textual annotations for text-to-makeup tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *unified* approach.  Existing makeup models generally tackle each task (transfer, removal, etc.) with separate architectures or require complex multi-module designs. MAD attempts to simplify this by training *one* diffusion model capable of *all* these tasks simply by changing domain embeddings. The integration of CAM is a nice, though incremental, improvement.  The introduction of the MT-Text dataset is a valuable contribution to the relatively under-explored text-to-makeup area. However, the underlying techniques (diffusion models, domain embeddings, masking) are all established; the novelty is in the clever application and integration of these techniques for this particular problem.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to streamline makeup applications development. A single model is easier to train, maintain, and deploy than multiple specialized models.  The text-to-makeup component, especially with the new MT-Text dataset, makes the technology more user-friendly and accessible.  The paper shows that a single model can achieve results comparable to, and in some cases exceeding, the performance of specialized models across a range of tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Unified framework:**  The central idea of using a single diffusion model for multiple tasks significantly reduces complexity compared to existing methods.
    *   **Text-to-makeup and MT-Text dataset:**  This addresses a real gap in the literature and provides a useful resource for future research.
    *   **Component Asynchronous Masking (CAM):** Addresses the issue of styles with smaller regions and provides significant improvement for detail.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper includes comprehensive experiments and ablation studies to demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework and its components on various datasets.
    *   The framework is effective to preserve the facial identity of the input image.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Technique:** While the integration is novel, the individual components (diffusion models, domain embeddings, basic masking) are not new. The main contribution is application in the makeup task and how the task is re-formulated.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models are inherently computationally expensive, which can be a limitation for real-time or mobile applications. The authors attempt to alleviate this with 'last-K step denoising,' but further speed optimizations would be valuable.
    *   **Limited Diversity in Data:** The models depend heavily on the datasets it is trained on and can be limited by its diversity. Future data could make the model better.
    *   **Failure Cases:** As reported by the authors, the failure cases are facial occlusions which can affect facial alignment, and generation of unnatural text-to-makeup styles when the prompts are unclear.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the framework can be further optimized for speed and memory usage, it could have a significant impact on the development and deployment of makeup applications. The MT-Text dataset could stimulate more research in text-guided makeup editing.

*   **Score Rationale:** Given the clever integration of existing techniques, the valuable contributions to the area of text-guided makeup style transformations, and the potential for streamlining makeup-application development, I'm assigning a score of **7**. It is a valuable contribution and provides a foundation for a new approach for makeup models in the future. The potential impact of streamlining development is significant, although the computational cost of the diffusion model is a challenge and the core concepts in the makeup task are incrementally added.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### GPG: A Simple and Strong Reinforcement Learning Baseline for Model Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02546v1)
- **Authors**: Xiangxiang Chu, Hailang Huang, Xiao Zhang, Fei Wei, Yong Wang
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement Learning (RL) can directly enhance the reasoning capabilities of large language models without extensive reliance on Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT). In this work, we revisit the traditional Policy Gradient (PG) mechanism and propose a minimalist RL approach termed Group Policy Gradient (GPG). Unlike conventional methods, GPG directly optimize the original RL objective, thus obviating the need for surrogate loss functions. As illustrated in our paper, by eliminating both the critic and reference models, and avoiding KL divergence constraints, our approach significantly simplifies the training process when compared to Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Our approach achieves superior performance without relying on auxiliary techniques or adjustments. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method not only reduces computational costs but also consistently outperforms GRPO across various unimodal and multimodal tasks. Our code is available at https://github.com/AMAP-ML/GPG.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Group Policy Gradient (GPG), a simplified reinforcement learning (RL) approach for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). GPG revisits traditional policy gradient methods, eliminating the need for surrogate loss functions, critic models, and reference models commonly used in other RL techniques like Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). By directly optimizing the original RL objective and using group-level reward normalization, GPG aims to reduce computational complexity and variance in policy gradient estimation.  The authors demonstrate through experiments on various unimodal and multimodal tasks that GPG achieves superior performance compared to GRPO, while also being more computationally efficient.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in its minimalist approach to RL for LLM reasoning.  While policy gradients themselves are a long-standing technique, the specific simplification of removing the critic and reference models, bypassing surrogate losses, and leveraging group-level normalization in the context of LLM reasoning is a worthwhile contribution.  However, the individual components (policy gradients, reward normalization) are not novel on their own. The innovation primarily comes from their combination in this particular way for this particular application. Compared to other efforts to simplify PPO such as ReMax, GRPO, DAPO and REINFORCE++, the authors have adopted a more radical approach, by removing both critic model and reference model.

* **Significance:**  The significance of the work stems from its potential to improve the scalability and efficiency of RL-based LLM training. The reduction in computational costs and the elimination of complex components like critic and reference models can make RL more accessible for training reasoning capabilities in larger LLMs. The demonstrated improvements in performance compared to GRPO on a range of tasks further solidify its significance. By proving the effectiveness of direct optimization of the original RL problem, the paper also challenges the status quo.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Simplicity:** The main strength of the paper is its simplicity. GPG is easier to implement and train than PPO and GRPO, which is a crucial factor for wider adoption.
    * **Performance:** The experimental results clearly show that GPG outperforms GRPO across a variety of tasks, including both unimodal (mathematical reasoning) and multimodal (visual reasoning, classification) scenarios.
    * **Computational Efficiency:** The paper emphasizes the computational benefits of GPG, which is essential for scaling RL to larger LLMs.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, with clear explanations of the method and experimental setup.
    * **Open-Sourced Code:** The availability of the code is a valuable contribution, enabling other researchers to reproduce and extend the work.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Comparison:** While GPG is shown to outperform GRPO, the comparison against a broader range of state-of-the-art RL methods for LLM reasoning could strengthen the paper further.
    * **Lack of Theoretical Analysis:**  A more in-depth theoretical analysis of the convergence properties and stability of GPG would be beneficial. While empirical results are strong, a theoretical grounding would increase confidence in the method.
    * **Evaluation on Extremely Large Models:** The paper acknowledges that the method has not been evaluated on extremely large models due to computational constraints. This is a significant limitation, as the performance of GPG on such models would be a key factor in determining its real-world applicability.
    * **Reward Design:** Reward function design for specific reasoning tasks is still challenging. If the GPG algorithm could be made more independent of specialized rewards, it would be even more novel.

* **Potential Influence:** GPG has the potential to influence future research in RL for LLMs by demonstrating the effectiveness of a minimalist approach. The simplicity and efficiency of GPG could encourage researchers to explore other simplified RL techniques. It could also encourage more research to use GPG in different applications.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

Here's the reasoning:

*   **+1** for the simplicity and elegance of the method.
*   **+1** for the clear and well-written paper.
*   **+1** for providing the code.
*   **+1** for the empirical results showcasing GPG's superiority over GRPO across different tasks.
*   **+1** for contributing to the trend of simplifying PPO algorithms.
*   **-1** for the limited scope of comparison against a broader range of SOTA RL methods.
*   **-1** for the lack of theoretical analysis of GPG's convergence properties.
*   **-1** for the lack of experiments on larger models.

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field. It's a good step forward in simplification of PPO-like algorithms. While the work has some limitations, the potential impact on scaling RL for LLM reasoning is significant.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring Individual Factors in the Adoption of LLMs for Specific Software Engineering Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02553v1)
- **Authors**: Stefano Lambiase, Gemma Catolino, Fabio Palomba, Filomena Ferrucci, Daniel Russo
- **Abstract**: The advent of Large Language Models (LLMs) is transforming software development, significantly enhancing software engineering processes. Research has explored their role within development teams, focusing on specific tasks such as artifact generation, decision-making support, and information retrieval. Despite the growing body of work on LLMs in software engineering, most studies have centered on broad adoption trends, neglecting the nuanced relationship between individual cognitive and behavioral factors and their impact on task-specific adoption. While factors such as perceived effort and performance expectancy have been explored at a general level, their influence on distinct software engineering tasks remains underexamined. This gap hinders the development of tailored LLM-based systems (e.g., Generative AI Agents) that align with engineers' specific needs and limits the ability of team leaders to devise effective strategies for fostering LLM adoption in targeted workflows. This study bridges this gap by surveying N=188 software engineers to test the relationship between individual attributes related to technology adoption and LLM adoption across five key tasks, using structural equation modeling (SEM). The Unified Theory of Acceptance and Use of Technology (UTAUT2) was applied to characterize individual adoption behaviors. The findings reveal that task-specific adoption is influenced by distinct factors, some of which negatively impact adoption when considered in isolation, underscoring the complexity of LLM integration in software engineering. To support effective adoption, this article provides actionable recommendations, such as seamlessly integrating LLMs into existing development environments and encouraging peer-driven knowledge sharing to enhance information retrieval.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Exploring Individual Factors in the Adoption of LLMs for Specific Software Engineering Tasks":

**Summary:**
